\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{j\in[0:n-1]^{d}}\sum_{k\in[0:n-1]^{d}}\int_{[-\pi,\pi]^{d}}x_{j}e^{-\mathbbm{i}(j-k)^{\top}\lambda}y_{k}\alpha(\lambda)d\lambda\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{[-\pi,\pi]^{d}}\left|\sum_{j\in[0:n-1]^{d}}x_{j}e^{\mathbbm{i}j^{\top}\lambda}\right|\cdot|\alpha(\lambda)|\cdot\left|\sum_{k\in[0:n-1]^{d}}e^{\mathbbm{i}k^{\top}\lambda}y_{k}\right|d\lambda

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\lambda\in[-\pi,\pi]^{d}}|\alpha(\lambda)|\cdot\left(\int_{[-\pi,\pi]^{d}}\left|\sum_{j\in[0:n-1]^{d}}x_{j}e^{\mathbbm{i}j^{\top}\lambda}\right|^{2}d\lambda\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\times\left(\int_{[-\pi,\pi]^{d}}\left|\sum_{k\in[0:n-1]^{d}}y_{k}e^{\mathbbm{i}k^{\top}\lambda}\right|^{2}d\lambda\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{d}\cdot\sup_{\lambda\in[-\pi,\pi]^{d}}|\alpha(\lambda)|\cdot\|x\|_{2}\cdot\|y\|_{2},

The second inequality follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the last equality follows by
Parseval’s identity, since the functions
\varphi_{k}(\lambda):=(2\pi)^{-d/2}e^{\mathbbm{i}k^{\top}\lambda},\ \lambda\in[-\pi,\pi],\ k\in[0:(n-1)]^{d}
,
are orthonormal in
L^{2}([-\pi,\pi]^{d};\mathbb{C})
.

(ii)

If
A_{n}
is also positive definite, then
A_{n}
is invertible.
Since
\|\cdot\|_{\rm op}
is the spectral norm, we have that

\|A_{n}^{-1}\|_{\rm op}=\max\sigma_{i}(A_{n}^{-1})=\max\frac{1}{\sigma_{i}(A_{n})}=\sup\Big\{\frac{1}{x^{\top}A_{n}x}:\ \|x\|_{2}=1,x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\Big\}

where the last equality follows from Rayleigh quotient optimization results, when
A_{n}
is positive definite.

As in part (1), with
z^{\star}
denoting the complex conjugate of
z
, we have that

\displaystyle|x^{\top}A_{n}x|

\displaystyle=x^{\top}A_{n}x=\int_{[-\pi,\pi]^{d}}\left(\sum_{j\in[0:n-1]^{d}}x_{j}e^{\mathbbm{i}j^{\top}\lambda}\right)\alpha(\lambda)\left(\sum_{k\in[0:n-1]^{d}}e^{\mathbbm{i}k^{\top}\lambda}y_{k}\right)d\lambda

\displaystyle=\int_{[-\pi,\pi]^{d}}\left|\sum_{j\in[0:n-1]^{d}}x_{j}e^{-\mathbbm{i}j^{\top}\lambda}\right|^{2}\alpha(\lambda)d\lambda

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{\lambda\in[-\pi,\pi]^{d}}|\alpha(\lambda)|\int_{[-\pi,\pi]^{d}}\left|\sum_{j\in[0:n-1]^{d}}x_{j}e^{-\mathbbm{i}j\lambda}\right|^{2}d\lambda

\displaystyle=\Big(\sup_{\lambda\in[-\pi,\pi]^{d}}|\alpha^{-1}(\lambda)|\Big)^{-1}\cdot\|x\|_{2}^{2}\cdot(2\pi)^{d},

and the result follows.

(iii)

It follows that

\|A_{n}\|_{\rm F}^{2}=\sum_{i\in[0:n-1]^{d}}\sum_{j\in[0:n-1]^{d}}|a_{i-j}|^{2}=\sum_{k\in[-(n-1):(n-1)]^{d}}\prod_{i=1}^{d}(n-|k_{i}|)|a_{k}|^{2},

where
[-(n-1):(n-1)]^{d}:=\{-(n-1),\cdots,n-1\}^{d}
.
Thus,

\frac{1}{n^{d}}\|A_{n}\|_{\rm F}^{2}=\sum_{k\in[-(n-1):(n-1)]^{d}}\prod_{i=1}^{d}\Big(1-\frac{|k_{i}|}{n}\Big)|a_{k}|^{2}\leq\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|a_{j}|^{2}=(2\pi)^{d}\int_{[-\pi,\pi]^{d}}\alpha^{2}(\lambda)d\lambda,

by Parseval’s identity.

∎

Lemma S.3.2
.

Let
B_{0}
and
B_{1}
be symmetric, positive definite
n\times n
matrices such that
D:=B_{1}-B_{0}
is
non-negative definite. Let
P_{0}
and
P_{1}
be the probability distributions of zero-mean Gaussian vectors with covariance matrices
B_{0}
and
B_{1}
, respectively.
Then, there is a
\xi\in[0,1]
such that

{\rm KL}(P_{1},P_{0})\leq\frac{1}{2}\|D\|_{\rm F}^{2}\|B_{\xi}^{-1}\|_{\rm op}^{2},

where
B_{\xi}:=B_{0}+\xi D
, and where
\|\cdot\|_{\rm F}
stands for the matrix Frobenius norm and
\|\cdot\|_{\rm op}
stands for the matrix operator norm induced by the Euclidean vector norm.

Since the data is assumed Gaussian, we can immediately obtain that

{\rm KL}(P_{1},P_{0})=\frac{1}{2}\left\{{\rm tr}(B_{1}B_{0}^{-1}-E)+\log|B_{0}|-\log|B_{1}|\right\},

(S.3.23)

where
{\rm tr}(A)
and
|A|
are the trace and determinant of the matrix
A
and
E
is the identity matrix of dimensions
n\times n
.
Notice that
B_{\lambda}:=B_{0}+\lambda D,\ \lambda\in[0,1]
is a positive definite covariance matrix. The expression in (
S.3.23
) can
be rewritten as

{\rm KL}(P_{1},P_{0})=\frac{1}{2}\left\{{\rm tr}[B_{1}(B_{0}^{-1}-B_{1}^{-1})]+\log|B_{0}|-\log|B_{1}|\right\}.

(S.3.24)

We define the function
\phi(\lambda)={\rm tr}(B_{1}B_{\lambda}^{-1})+\log|B_{\lambda}|
and note that
by the intermediate value theorem, we have

{\rm KL}(P_{1},P_{0})=\frac{\phi(0)-\phi(1)}{2}=-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\phi^{\prime}(\xi),

for some
\xi\in[0,1]
. Using the following differentiation rules

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\lambda}A^{-1}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=-A^{-1}(\lambda)\left(\frac{d}{d\lambda}A(\lambda)\right)A^{-1}(\lambda),

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\lambda}\log|A(\lambda)|

\displaystyle={\rm tr}\left(A^{-1}(\lambda)\frac{d}{d\lambda}A(\lambda)\right),

and the fact that
dB_{\lambda}/d\lambda=D
, we obtain that (
S.3.24
) becomes:

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\rm KL}(P_{1},P_{0})&=\frac{1}{2}\cdot{\rm tr}\left[B_{1}B_{\xi}^{-1}DB_{\xi}^{-1}-DB_{\xi}^{-1}\right]\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\cdot{\rm tr}\left[(B_{1}-B_{\xi})B_{\xi}^{-1}DB_{\xi}^{-1}\right],\end{split}

(S.3.25)

for some
\xi\in[0,1]
. To estimate the rhs of (
S.3.25
) we will use the following inequalities
(
Davies 1973
, see, e.g.,)
:

\displaystyle|{\rm tr}(AB)|\leq\|A\|_{\rm F}\cdot\|B\|_{\rm F}.

(S.3.26)

If the matrices
A
and
B
are symmetric, then

\displaystyle\|BA\|_{\rm F}=\|AB\|_{\rm F}\leq\|A\|_{\rm op}\|B\|_{\rm F},

(S.3.27)

where

\|A\|_{\rm F}=\left(\sum_{i,j}a_{ij}^{2}\right)^{1/2},\ \ \|A\|_{\rm op}=\sup\{\|Ax\|_{2};\ \|x\|_{2}=1,\ x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\}.

From (
S.3.25
) with the help of (
S.3.26
) and (
S.3.27
) we obtain:

\displaystyle{\rm KL}(P_{1},P_{0})\leq\frac{1}{2}\|B_{1}-B_{\xi}\|_{\rm F}\|D\|_{\rm F}\|B_{\xi}^{-1}\|_{\rm op}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\|D\|_{\rm F}^{2}\|B_{\xi}^{-1}\|_{\rm op}^{2}.

∎

S.4
Proofs for Section
7

This section provides the proofs for Theorem
7.1
. As stated in Section
7
,
\{X(t),t\in\mathbb{R}\}
is a stationary Gaussian process in the complex Hilbert space
\mathbb{H}
, we observe
X
at
t=k\delta_{n},k=1,\ldots,n
, where
n\delta_{n}\to\infty
. We will focus on the case
\delta_{n}\equiv 1
, which contains
the main ideas of the proof. The proof for the general case follows from a straightforward adaptation of the special case.

S.4.1
An overview of the proof of Theorem
7.1

As mentioned, we focus on the case
\delta_{n}\equiv 1
, which is essentially the time-series setting (cf. Section
5.1
). Thus, we consider a discrete-time stationary process
X=\{X(t),t\in\mathbb{Z}\},
where
X(t)
are Gaussian elements of the complex Hilbert space
\mathbb{H}
.
We explained in Section
7
of the paper that the conditions (a)-(f) in Assumption
7
hold in this case if

\displaystyle\sum_{x=-\infty}^{\infty}\left[\|C(x)\|_{\rm tr}+\|\check{C}(x)\|_{\rm tr}\right]<\infty,

(S.4.1)

which we assume below. Note that
C
and
\check{C}
are defined in (
2.2
) and (
2.4
) respectively.
Recall that
\mathbb{X}
denotes the Hilbert space of Hilbert-Schmidt operators
{\cal A}:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{H}
, equipped with the HS-inner product

\langle{\cal A},{\cal B}\rangle_{\rm HS}:={\rm trace}({\cal B}^{*}{\cal A}),\ {\cal A},\ {\cal B}\in\mathbb{X}
, and corresponding norm

\|{\cal A}\|_{\rm HS}=\langle{\cal A},{\cal A}\rangle_{\rm HS}^{1/2}
. The spectral and pseudo spectral density functions in this case are given by

\displaystyle f(\theta)=\frac{\delta}{2\pi}\sum_{k=-\infty}^{\infty}e^{\mathbbm{i}k^{\top}\theta\delta}C(k),\ \check{f}(\theta)=\frac{\delta}{2\pi}\sum_{k=-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-\mathbbm{i}k^{\top}\theta\delta}\check{C}(k),\ \theta\in[-\pi,\pi].

Also,

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}_{n}(\theta):=\sqrt{\frac{n}{\Delta_{n}}}\left(\hat{f}_{n}(\theta)-\mathbb{E}\hat{f}_{n}(\theta)\right),

where

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{n}(\theta)=\frac{1}{2\pi n}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}e^{\mathbbm{i}(i-j)\theta}X(i)\otimes X(j)K\left(\frac{i-j}{\Delta_{n}}\right),\ \theta\in[-\pi,\pi].

For this special setting, we will establish the following theorem:

Theorem S.4.1
.

Let
X=\{X(t),t\in\mathbb{Z}\}
be a stationary Gaussian process of the complex Hilbert space
\mathbb{H}
. Let
\Delta_{n}\to\infty,\Delta_{n}/n\to 0
, and
assume that (
S.4.1
) and Assumption
7
hold.
Define

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}_{n}(\theta):=\sqrt{\frac{n}{\Delta_{n}}}\left[\hat{f}_{n}(\theta)-\mathbb{E}\hat{f}_{n}(\theta)\right],\ \ \theta\in\mathbb{R},

where
\hat{f}_{n}(\theta)
is given in (
7.1
).
Then, for any
\theta\in[-\pi,\pi]
,

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}_{n}(\theta)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathscr{T}(\theta)\ \mbox{ in $\mathbb{X}$},

where
\mathscr{T}(\theta)
is a zero-mean Gaussian element of
\mathbb{X}
, such that for every finite collection
\{g_{\ell},\ell=1,\ldots,m\},
and positive numbers
\{a_{\ell},\ell=1,\ldots,m\},

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\rm Var}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}a_{\ell}\left\langle\mathscr{T}(\theta)g_{\ell},g_{\ell}\right\rangle\right)\\
&=\|K\|_{2}^{2}\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}=1}^{m}a_{\ell_{1}}a_{\ell_{2}}\left[\left|\left\langle f(\theta)g_{\ell_{2}},g_{\ell_{1}}\right\rangle\right|^{2}+I_{(\theta=0,\pm\pi)}\left|\left\langle\check{f}(\theta)\overline{g_{\ell_{2}}},g_{\ell_{1}}\right\rangle\right|^{2}\right].\end{split}

The following proposition describes the roadmap for proving this result. For simplicity of notation, we will henceforth suppress the argument
\theta
in
\mathscr{T}_{n}(\theta)
since it is fixed.

Proposition S.4.2
.

Let the assumptions of Theorem
S.4.1
hold. Also,
let
\{e_{i},i\geq 1\}
be a CONS of
\mathbb{H}
.
Assume that

(i)

for any
\epsilon,\delta>0
, there exists
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{n\geq 1}\mathbb{P}(\|({\rm I}-\Pi_{u})\mathscr{T}_{n}\|_{\rm HS}>\epsilon)<\delta,

where
\Pi_{u}:\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
is the orthogonal projection operator on
\mathbb{X}_{u}:={\rm span}(e_{i}\otimes e_{j},i,j\leq u)
, and

(ii)

for all
a_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}
and
g_{\ell}\in\mathbb{H}
,
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}a_{\ell}\left\langle\mathscr{T}_{n}g_{\ell},g_{\ell}\right\rangle\right]^{k}\sim\begin{cases}\mathscr{O}\left(\left(\frac{\Delta_{n}}{n}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)&,\ k\quad\mbox{odd},\\
(k-1)!!\left[\sigma_{a,g}^{2}\right]^{\frac{k}{2}}&,\ k\quad\mbox{even},\end{cases}

(S.4.2)

where

\sigma_{a,g}^{2}:=\|K\|_{2}^{2}\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}=1}^{m}a_{\ell_{1}}a_{\ell_{2}}\left(\left|\left\langle f(\theta)g_{\ell_{1}},g_{\ell_{2}}\right\rangle\right|^{2}+1_{\{0,\pm\pi\}}(\theta)\left|\left\langle\check{f}(\theta)\overline{g_{\ell_{1}}},g_{\ell_{2}}\right\rangle\right|^{2}\right).

Then there exists a Gaussian process
\mathscr{T}
in
\mathbb{X}
that fulfills the description of Theorem
S.4.1
.

First, we state a useful identity for a complex Hilbert space.
Write

\langle\mathscr{T}_{n},e_{i}\otimes e_{j}\rangle_{\rm HS}=\langle\mathscr{T}_{n}e_{j},e_{i}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}=:\mathscr{T}_{n}(e_{j},e_{i}),

By Lemma A.8 of
Shen, Stoev and Hsing 2022
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathscr{T}_{n}(e_{j},e_{i})=&\frac{\mathbbm{i}-1}{2}(\mathscr{T}_{n}(e_{j},e_{j})+\mathscr{T}_{n}(e_{i},e_{i}))+\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{T}_{n}(e_{j}+e_{i},e_{j}+e_{i})\\
&-\frac{\mathbbm{i}}{2}\mathscr{T}_{n}(\mathbbm{i}e_{j}+e_{i},\mathbbm{i}e_{j}+e_{i}).\end{split}

(S.4.3)

Also, recall that
\{e_{i}\otimes e_{j},i,j\geq 1\}
is a CONS of
\mathbb{X}
. Thus, (i) implies the flat concentration condition of Condition 1 of Theorem 7.7.4 of
Hsing and Eubank 2015
. It follows from (ii), applying (
S.4.3
) plus Markov’s inequality, that Condition 2 of Theorem 7.7.4 of
Hsing and Eubank 2015
also holds. Thus,
\{\mathscr{T}_{n},n\geq 1\}
is tight and hence relatively compact. To show that
\mathscr{T}_{n}
converges in distribution to some
\mathscr{T}
, it suffices to show that if
\mathscr{T}_{n^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathscr{T}
along some subsequence
\{n^{\prime}\}
, then
\mathscr{T}
does not depend on the subsequence. Now, by the continuous mapping theorem, (
S.4.2
) and a standard uniform integrability argument, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}a_{\ell}\left\langle\mathscr{T}_{n^{\prime}}g_{\ell},g_{\ell}\right\rangle\right]^{k}\to\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}a_{\ell}\left\langle\mathscr{T}g_{\ell},g_{\ell}\right\rangle\right]^{k}\ \ \mbox{for all $k$},

where the limiting moments entail that

\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}a_{\ell}\langle\mathscr{T}g_{\ell},g_{\ell}\rangle
is distributed as
N(0,\sigma_{a,g}^{2})

(
Billingsley 2012
, cf.
Theorem 30.1 of)
.
Relation (
S.4.3
) shows that the finite-dimensional distributions
of the real Gaussian process
\{\langle\mathscr{T}g_{\ell},g_{\ell}\rangle,\ g_{\ell}\in\mathbb{H}\}
determine the finite-dimensional distributions of
\{\langle\mathscr{T}g,h\rangle,\ g,h\in\mathbb{H}\}
, which in turn characterize the law of the
\mathbb{X}
-valued random element
\mathscr{T}
.
The result thus follows.
∎

We will complete the proof of Theorem
S.4.1
by verifying the conditions (i) and (ii) of Proposition
S.4.2
, which will be established in Sections
S.4.2
and
S.4.3
, respectively.

S.4.2
Verifying (i) of Proposition
S.4.2

In this subsection, we establish that (i) of Proposition
S.4.2
holds for
\{\mathscr{T}_{n}\}
under the assumptions of the Proposition. This property is
known as the flat concentration of
\{\mathscr{T}_{n}\}
.

By Markov’s inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\|({\rm I}-\Pi_{u})\mathscr{T}_{n}\|_{\rm HS}>\epsilon)\leq\epsilon^{-2}\mathbb{E}\|({\rm I}-\Pi_{u})\mathscr{T}_{n}\|_{\rm HS}^{2}.

Since

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|({\rm I}-\Pi_{u})\mathscr{T}_{n}\|_{\rm HS}^{2}=\sum_{k\vee\ell>u}\mathbb{E}|\langle\mathscr{T}_{n},e_{k}\otimes e_{\ell}\rangle_{\rm HS}|^{2},

(S.4.4)

it is sufficient to show that

\displaystyle\sum_{k,\ell}\sup_{n}\mathbb{E}\left|\left\langle\mathscr{T}_{n},e_{k}\otimes e_{\ell}\right\rangle_{\rm HS}\right|^{2}<\infty,

(S.4.5)

which implies (i) of Proposition
S.4.2
by (
S.4.4
).

Without loss of generality suppose that the CONS
\{e_{j},\ j\in\mathbb{N}\}
of
\mathbb{H}
is
real
and thus

X(t)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}X_{j}(t)e_{j}
, where
X_{j}(t)=\langle X(t),e_{j}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}
are complex zero-mean Gaussian random variables.
Also, let

\displaystyle C_{k,\ell}(x)=\langle C(x),e_{k}\otimes e_{\ell}\rangle_{\rm HS}=\langle C(x)e_{\ell},e_{k}\rangle.

(S.4.6)

It follows that

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathscr{T}_{n},e_{k}\otimes e_{\ell}\right\rangle_{\rm HS}

\displaystyle=\frac{(2\pi)^{-1}}{\sqrt{n\Delta_{n}}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\left\langle X(i)\otimes X(j)-C(i-j),e_{k}\otimes e_{\ell}\right\rangle_{\rm HS}e^{\mathbbm{i}\theta(i-j)}K\left(\frac{i-j}{\Delta_{n}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{(2\pi)^{-1}}{\sqrt{n\Delta_{n}}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\left[X_{k}(i)\overline{X_{\ell}(j)}-C_{k,\ell}(i-j)\right]e^{\mathbbm{i}\theta(i-j)}K\left(\frac{i-j}{\Delta_{n}}\right),

where
C_{k,\ell}(i-j)=\mathbb{E}[X_{k}(i)\overline{X_{\ell}(j)}].

By Lemma
S.5.3
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[X_{k}(i_{1})\overline{X_{\ell}(j_{1})X_{k}(i_{2})}X_{\ell}(j_{2})]

\displaystyle=C_{k,\ell}(i_{1}-j_{1})\overline{C_{k,\ell}(i_{2}-j_{2})}+\check{C}_{k,\ell}(i_{1}-j_{2})\overline{\check{C}_{k,\ell}(i_{2}-j_{1})}

\displaystyle+C_{k,k}(i_{1}-i_{2})\overline{C_{\ell,\ell}(j_{1}-j_{2})}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}\left|\left\langle\mathscr{T}_{n},e_{k}\otimes e_{\ell}\right\rangle_{\rm HS}\right|^{2}\\
&=\frac{(2\pi)^{-2}}{n\Delta_{n}}\sum_{i_{1},j_{1}}\sum_{i_{2},j_{2}}e^{\mathbbm{i}\theta(i_{1}-j_{1}-i_{2}+j_{2})}K\left(\frac{i_{1}-j_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)K\left(\frac{i_{2}-j_{2}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)\\
&\quad\times\mathbb{E}\left\{\left[X_{k}(i_{1})\overline{X_{\ell}(j_{1})}-C_{k,\ell}(i_{1}-j_{1})\right]\left[\overline{X_{k}(i_{2})}X_{\ell}(j_{2})-\overline{C_{k,\ell}(i_{2}-j_{2})}\right]\right\}\\
&=\frac{(2\pi)^{-2}}{n\Delta_{n}}\sum_{i_{1},j_{1}}\sum_{i_{2},j_{2}}e^{\mathbbm{i}\theta(i_{1}-j_{1}-i_{2}+j_{2})}K\left(\frac{i_{1}-j_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)K\left(\frac{i_{2}-j_{2}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times C_{k,k}(i_{1}-i_{2})\overline{C_{\ell,\ell}(j_{1}-j_{2})}\\
&\quad+\frac{(2\pi)^{-2}}{n\Delta_{n}}\sum_{i_{1},j_{1}}\sum_{i_{2},j_{2}}e^{\mathbbm{i}\theta(i_{1}-j_{1}-i_{2}+j_{2})}K\left(\frac{i_{1}-j_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)K\left(\frac{i_{2}-j_{2}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\check{C}_{k,\ell}(i_{1}-j_{2})\overline{\check{C}_{k,\ell}(i_{2}-j_{1})}\\
&=:A_{k,\ell}+B_{k,\ell}.\end{split}

(S.4.7)

We start with
A_{k,\ell}
. With the change of variables

\displaystyle x_{1}=i_{1}-i_{2},

\displaystyle\ \ x_{2}=j_{1}-j_{2},

\displaystyle y_{1}=i_{1}-j_{1},

\displaystyle\ \ y_{2}=i_{1},

we obtain

\displaystyle A_{k,\ell}

\displaystyle=\frac{(2\pi)^{-2}}{n\Delta_{n}}\sum_{x_{1},x_{2}=1-n}^{n-1}C_{k,k}(x_{1})\overline{C_{\ell,\ell}(x_{2})}e^{\mathbbm{i}\theta(x_{1}-x_{2})}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\sum_{y_{1}=(-\Delta_{n})\vee(1-n+x_{1}-x_{2})}^{\Delta_{n}\wedge(n-1+x_{1}-x_{2})}K\left(\frac{y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)K\left(\frac{-x_{1}+x_{2}+y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)\sum_{y_{2}=1\vee(1+y_{1})}^{n\wedge(n+y_{1})}1

\displaystyle=\frac{(2\pi)^{-2}}{n\Delta_{n}}\sum_{x_{1},x_{2}=1-n}^{n-1}C_{k,k}(x_{1})\overline{C_{\ell,\ell}(x_{2})}e^{\mathbbm{i}\theta(x_{1}-x_{2})}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\sum_{y_{1}=(-\Delta_{n})\vee(1-n+x_{1}-x_{2})}^{\Delta_{n}\wedge(n-1+x_{1}-x_{2})}K\left(\frac{y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)K\left(\frac{-x_{1}+x_{2}+y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)({n-|y_{1}|}).

Thus, with
\|K\|_{\infty}:=\max_{t}|K(t)|
, we obtain

\displaystyle|A_{k,\ell}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|K\|_{\infty}^{2}}{2\pi^{2}}\sum_{x_{1},x_{2}=1-n}^{n-1}|C_{k,k}(x_{1})||C_{\ell,\ell}(x_{2})|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|K\|_{\infty}^{2}}{2\pi^{2}}\sum_{x_{1},x_{2}=-\infty}^{\infty}|C_{k,k}(x_{1})||C_{\ell,\ell}(x_{2})|=:\alpha_{k,\ell}.

By (
S.4.6
) and (ii) of Lemma
S.7.1
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k,\ell}\alpha_{k,\ell}\leq\frac{\|K\|_{\infty}^{2}}{2\pi^{2}}\left(\sum_{x=-\infty}^{\infty}\|C(x)\|_{\rm tr}\right)^{2}<\infty.

(S.4.8)

We now turn to
B_{k,\ell}
in (
S.4.7
). With the change of variables

\displaystyle x_{1}=i_{1}-j_{2},

\displaystyle\ \ x_{2}=i_{2}-j_{1},

\displaystyle y_{1}=i_{1}-j_{1},

\displaystyle\ \ y_{2}=i_{1},

\displaystyle B_{k,\ell}

\displaystyle=\frac{(2\pi)^{-2}}{n\Delta_{n}}\sum_{x_{1},x_{2}=1-n}^{n-1}\check{C}_{k,\ell}(x_{1})\overline{\check{C}_{k,\ell}(x_{2})}e^{\mathbbm{i}\theta(-x_{1}-x_{2}+2y_{1})}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\sum_{y_{1}=(-\Delta_{n})\vee(1-n+x_{1}+x_{2})}^{\Delta_{n}\wedge(n-1+x_{1}+x_{2})}K\left(\frac{y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)K\left(\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}-y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)\sum_{y_{2}=1\vee(1+y_{1})}^{n\wedge(n+y_{1})}1

\displaystyle=\frac{(2\pi)^{-2}}{n\Delta_{n}}\sum_{x_{1},x_{2}=1-n}^{n-1}\check{C}_{k,\ell}(x_{1})\overline{\check{C}_{k,\ell}(x_{2})}e^{\mathbbm{i}\theta(-x_{1}-x_{2}+2y_{1})}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\sum_{y_{1}=(-\Delta_{n})\vee(1-n+x_{1}+x_{2})}^{\Delta_{n}\wedge(n-1+x_{1}+x_{2})}K\left(\frac{y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)K\left(\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}-y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)({n-|y_{1}|}).

Thus,

\displaystyle|B_{k,\ell}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|K\|_{\infty}^{2}}{2\pi^{2}}\sum_{x_{1},x_{2}=1-n}^{n-1}\left|\check{C}_{k,\ell}(x_{1})\right|\left|\check{C}_{k,\ell}(x_{2})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|K\|_{\infty}^{2}}{2\pi^{2}}\sum_{x_{1},x_{2}=1-n}^{n-1}\left|\check{C}_{k,\ell}(x_{1})\right|\left|\check{C}_{k,\ell}(x_{2})\right|=:\beta_{k,\ell}.

Applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and in view of the definition of the Hilbert-Schmidt inner product,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{k,\ell}\beta_{k,\ell}&\leq\frac{\|K\|_{\infty}^{2}}{2\pi^{2}}\sum_{x_{1},x_{2}=-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{k,\ell}\left|\check{C}_{k,\ell}(x_{1})\right|\left|\check{C}_{k,\ell}(x_{2})\right|\\
&\leq\frac{\|K\|_{\infty}^{2}}{2\pi^{2}}\sum_{x_{1},x_{2}=-\infty}^{\infty}\sqrt{\sum_{k,\ell}\left|\check{C}_{k,\ell}(x_{1})\right|^{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{k,\ell}\left|\check{C}_{k,\ell}(x_{2})\right|^{2}}\\
&=\frac{\|K\|_{\infty}^{2}}{2\pi^{2}}\left(\sum_{x=-\infty}^{\infty}{\|\check{C}(x)\|_{\rm HS}}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{\|K\|_{\infty}^{2}}{2\pi^{2}}\left(\sum_{x=-\infty}^{\infty}{\|\check{C}(x)\|_{\rm tr}}\right)^{2}<\infty,\end{split}

(S.4.9)

Since the upper bounds
\alpha_{k,\ell}
and
\beta_{k,\ell}
do not depend on
n
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{n}\mathbb{E}\left|\left\langle\mathscr{T}_{n},e_{k}\otimes e_{\ell}\right\rangle_{\rm HS}\right|^{2}\leq\alpha_{k,\ell}+\beta_{k,\ell},

and Relations (
S.4.8
) and (
S.4.9
) imply (
S.4.5
).

S.4.3
Verifying (ii) of Proposition
S.4.2

The proof of (ii) is quite lengthy, and constitutes the core of the central limit theorem proof. In Section
S.4.3.1
, we will first focus on showing (ii) for the case that
X
is scalar, i.e.,
X
take values in
\mathbb{C}
. There we will take advantage of this simple setting to explain the ideas of the proof. In Section
S.4.3.2
, we will prove the proposition for the general case of
X\in\mathbb{H}
.

S.4.3.1
The scalar case

In this section, we focus on a zero-mean, stationary Gaussian time-series taking values in
\mathbb{C}
and compute the moments of
\mathscr{T}_{n}
. The purpose of this section is to develop
technical tools for the general moment calculations needed to prove (ii).

In this setting,
C(t-s)=\mathbb{E}[X(t)\overline{X(s)}]
,
\check{C}(t-s):=\mathbb{E}[X(t)X(s)]
, and

f(\theta)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{x=-\infty}^{\infty}C(x)e^{\mathbbm{i}x\theta}.

(S.4.10)

Also, (
S.4.1
) becomes

\sum_{x=-\infty}^{\infty}\Big[|C(x)|+|\check{C}(x)|\Big]<\infty.

(S.4.11)

Observe that since
C(x)=\overline{C(-x)}
in this case, we have that
f(\theta)
is real, even though the process
\{X_{t},\ t\in\mathbb{Z}\}
is
complex-valued. We shall also need the so-called pseudo-spectral density, defined as

\check{f}(\theta)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{x=-\infty}^{\infty}\check{C}(x)e^{-\mathbbm{i}x\theta}.

(S.4.12)

Recall also that the spectral density estimator is

\hat{f}_{n}(\theta)=\frac{1}{2\pi n}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}X_{i}\overline{X_{j}}K_{\theta}(i-j),

where

K_{\theta}(y)=e^{\mathbbm{i}y\theta}K(y/\Delta_{n}).

The following proposition gives the asymptotic expression of
\mathbb{E}(\mathscr{T}_{n}^{k})
for the scalar case.

Proposition S.4.3
.

Assume that the conditions of Proposition
S.4.2
hold for the setting
\mathbb{H}=\mathbb{C}
.
Then, as
n\to\infty
,

\mathbb{E}(\mathscr{T}_{n}^{k})=\begin{cases}\mathscr{O}\left(\left(\frac{\Delta_{n}}{n}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)&,\ k\quad\mbox{odd},\\
(1+o(1))(k-1)!!\left[(f(\theta)^{2}+1_{\{0,\pm\pi\}}(\theta)|\check{f}(\theta)|^{2})\|K\|_{2}^{2}\right]^{\frac{k}{2}}&,\ k\quad\mbox{even},\end{cases}

where
f(\theta)
and
\check{f}(\theta)
are as in (
S.4.10
) and (
S.4.12
).

Assume without loss of generality that the support of
K
is
[-1,1]
.

The case
k=2

By Lemma
S.5.4
(for
N=0,\ M=2
, i.e., see (
S.5.3
)),

\displaystyle(2\pi)^{2}\mathbb{E}(\mathscr{T}_{n}^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n\Delta_{n}}\sum_{i_{1},j_{1}=1}^{n}\sum_{i_{2},j_{2}=1}^{n}K_{\theta}\left(i_{1}-j_{1}\right)K_{\theta}\left(i_{2}-j_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\mathbb{E}\left[\left(X_{i_{1}}\overline{X_{j_{1}}}-C(i_{1}-j_{1})\right)\left(X_{i_{2}}\overline{X_{j_{2}}}-C(i_{2}-j_{2})\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n\Delta_{n}}\sum_{i_{1},j_{1}=1}^{n}\sum_{i_{2},j_{2}=1}^{n}K_{\theta}\left(i_{1}-j_{1}\right)K_{\theta}\left(i_{2}-j_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\left[C(i_{1}-j_{2})C(i_{2}-j_{1})+\check{C}(i_{1}-i_{2})\overline{\check{C}(j_{1}-j_{2})}\right]

\displaystyle=:\widetilde{A}_{2}+\overline{A}_{2},

where

\widetilde{A}_{2}:=\frac{1}{n\Delta_{n}}\sum_{i_{1},j_{1}=1}^{n}\sum_{i_{2},j_{2}=1}^{n}K_{\theta}\left(i_{1}-j_{1}\right)K_{\theta}\left(i_{2}-j_{2}\right)\cdot C(i_{1}-j_{2})C(i_{2}-j_{1}),

and

\overline{A}_{2}:=\frac{1}{n\Delta_{n}}\sum_{i_{1},j_{1}=1}^{n}\sum_{i_{2},j_{2}=1}^{n}K_{\theta}\left(i_{1}-j_{1}\right)K_{\theta}\left(i_{2}-j_{2}\right)\cdot\check{C}(i_{1}-i_{2})\overline{\check{C}(j_{1}-j_{2})}.

The two terms have somewhat different properties and we start with
\widetilde{A}_{2}
.
With the change of variables

\displaystyle x_{1}

\displaystyle=i_{1}-j_{2},\quad x_{2}=i_{2}-j_{1},

\displaystyle y_{1}

\displaystyle=i_{1}-j_{1},\quad y_{2}=i_{1},

we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n\Delta_{n}}\sum_{x_{1},x_{2}=-n+1}^{n-1}C(x_{1})C(x_{2})e^{\mathbbm{i}x_{1}\theta}e^{\mathbbm{i}x_{2}\theta}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\sum_{y_{1}=(1-n)\vee(1-n+x_{1}+x_{2})}^{(n-1)\wedge(n-1+x_{1}+x_{2})}K\left(\frac{y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)K\left(\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}-y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)\sum_{y_{2}=1\vee(1+y_{1})}^{n\wedge(n+y_{1})}1

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n\Delta_{n}}\sum_{x_{1},x_{2}=-n+1}^{n-1}C(x_{1})C(x_{2})e^{\mathbbm{i}x_{1}\theta}e^{\mathbbm{i}x_{2}\theta}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\sum_{y_{1}=(1-n)\vee(1-n+x_{1}+x_{2})}^{(n-1)\wedge(n-1+x_{1}+x_{2})}K\left(\frac{y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)K\left(\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}-y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)\cdot({n-|y_{1}|})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Delta_{n}}\sum_{|x_{1}|\vee|x_{2}|\leq L}C(x_{1})C(x_{2})e^{\mathbbm{i}x_{1}\theta}e^{\mathbbm{i}x_{2}\theta}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\sum_{y_{1}=(1-n)\vee(1-n+x_{1}+x_{2})}^{(n-1)\wedge(n-1+x_{1}+x_{2})}K\left(\frac{y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)K\left(\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}-y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)\frac{{n-|y_{1}|}}{n}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\Delta_{n}}\sum_{|x_{1}|\vee|x_{2}|\geq L}C(x_{1})C(x_{2})e^{\mathbbm{i}x_{1}\theta}e^{\mathbbm{i}x_{2}\theta}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\sum_{y_{1}=(1-n)\vee(1-n+x_{1}+x_{2})}^{(n-1)\wedge(n-1+x_{1}+x_{2})}K\left(\frac{y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)K\left(\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}-y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)\frac{{n-|y_{1}|}}{n}

\displaystyle=:B_{1}+B_{2},

for some
L=L_{n}\to\infty
and
L=o(\Delta_{n}).

One can easily see that

\displaystyle|B_{2}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|K\|_{\infty}}{\Delta_{n}}\sum_{|x_{1}|\vee|x_{2}|\geq L}|C(x_{1})||C(x_{2})|\sum_{y_{1}=-n+1}^{n-1}K\left(\frac{y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\|K\|_{\infty}\sum_{|x_{1}|\vee|x_{2}|\geq L}|C(x_{1})||C(x_{2})|=o\left(1\right),\ \mbox{as}\ L\to\infty,

by (
S.4.11
).
Now, adding and subtracting the same term in
B_{1}
, one obtains that
B_{1}=C_{1}+C_{2},
where

\displaystyle C_{1}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\Delta_{n}}\sum_{|x_{1}|\vee|x_{2}|\leq L}C(x_{1})C(x_{2})e^{\mathbbm{i}x_{1}\theta}e^{\mathbbm{i}x_{2}\theta}\sum_{y_{1}=(1-n)\vee(1-n+x_{1}+x_{2})}^{(n-1)\wedge(n-1+x_{1}+x_{2})}K^{2}\left(\frac{y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)\frac{{n-|y_{1}|}}{n}

and

\displaystyle C_{2}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\Delta_{n}}\sum_{|x_{1}|\vee|x_{2}|\leq L}C(x_{1})C(x_{2})e^{\mathbbm{i}x_{1}\theta}e^{\mathbbm{i}x_{2}\theta}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\sum_{y_{1}=(1-n)\vee(1-n+x_{1}+x_{2})}^{(n-1)\wedge(n-1+x_{1}+x_{2})}K\left(\frac{y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)\left[K\left(\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}-y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)-K\left(\frac{y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)\right]\times\frac{{n-|y_{1}|}}{n}.

We examine
C_{1}
first.
Observe first that the inner sum over
y_{1}
is confined to

-\Delta_{n}\leq y_{1}\leq\Delta_{n}
, since
K
is supported on
[-1,1]
. Moreover, since
L=o(\Delta_{n})
and
\Delta_{n}=o(n)
,
for all
|x_{1}|\vee|x_{2}|\leq L
, and all sufficiently large
n
, we have that
(1-n)\vee(1-n+x_{1}+x_{2})\leq-\Delta_{n}
and

\Delta_{n}\leq(n-1)\wedge(n-1+x_{1}+x_{2})
. This means, that the inner summation in the definitions of
C_{1}
and
C_{2}

is over the range
[-\Delta_{n},\Delta_{n}]
and it does not depend on
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
. That is, for all sufficiently large
n
,

\displaystyle C_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{|x_{1}|\vee|x_{2}|\leq L}C(x_{1})C(x_{2})e^{\mathbbm{i}x_{1}\theta}e^{\mathbbm{i}x_{2}\theta}\times\frac{1}{\Delta_{n}}\sum_{y_{1}=-\Delta_{n}}^{\Delta_{n}}K^{2}\Big(\frac{y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\Big)

\displaystyle\sim 4\pi^{2}f(\theta)^{2}\int_{-1}^{1}K^{2}(y)dy,\ \ \mbox{ as }n\to\infty,

where the last relation follows from the Riemann integrability of
K^{2}
and the fact that
\sum_{|x|\leq L}C(x)e^{\mathbbm{i}x\theta}\to 2\pi f(\theta)
,
as
L\to\infty
.

Now, focus on the term
C_{2}
. Using the facts that
K
is an even function and

|K(x)-K(y)|\leq c|x-y|,

(since
K^{\prime}
is bounded), we get
|K((x_{1}+x_{2}-y_{1})/\Delta_{n})-K(y_{1}/\Delta_{n})|\leq 2cL/\Delta
, for all
|x_{1}|\vee|x_{2}|\leq L
.
Thus, by Condition (
S.4.11
) and the Riemann integrability of
K
, we obtain

\displaystyle|C_{2}|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{|x|\leq L}|C(x)|\right)^{2}\frac{1}{\Delta_{n}}\sum_{y=-\Delta_{n}}^{\Delta_{n}}K\left(\frac{y}{\Delta_{n}}\right)\frac{2Lc}{\Delta_{n}}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{2Lc}{\Delta_{n}}\left(\sum_{x=-\infty}^{\infty}|C(x)|\right)^{2}\int_{u=-1}^{1}K(u)du=o\left(1\right),

since
L=o(\Delta_{n})
.
Summarizing, we have that for all
\theta
(including
\theta=0
and
\theta=\pm\pi
)

\widetilde{A}_{2}=C_{1}+C_{2}+B_{2}\sim 4\pi^{2}f(\theta)^{2}\int_{-1}^{1}K^{2}(u)du.

We next consider
\overline{A}_{2}
. Similar to the derivation for
\widetilde{A}_{2}
, with the change of variables

\displaystyle x_{1}

\displaystyle=i_{1}-i_{2},\quad x_{2}=j_{1}-j_{2},

\displaystyle y_{1}

\displaystyle=i_{1}-j_{1},\quad y_{2}=i_{1},

we get

\displaystyle\overline{A}_{2}=\frac{1}{n\Delta_{n}}\sum_{x_{1},x_{2}=-n+1}^{n-1}\check{C}(x_{1})\overline{\check{C}(x_{2})}e^{-\mathbbm{i}x_{1}\theta}e^{\mathbbm{i}x_{2}\theta}

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\sum_{y_{1}=(1-n)\vee(1-n+x_{1}-x_{2})}^{(n-1)\wedge(n-1+x_{1}-x_{2})}K\left(\frac{y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)K\left(\frac{-x_{1}+x_{2}+y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)e^{\mathbbm{i}2y_{1}\theta}\sum_{y_{2}=1\vee(1+y_{1})}^{n\wedge(n+y_{1})}1

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Delta_{n}}\sum_{x_{1},x_{2}=-n+1}^{n-1}\check{C}(x_{1})\overline{\check{C}(x_{2})}e^{-\mathbbm{i}x_{1}\theta}e^{\mathbbm{i}x_{2}\theta}

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\sum_{y_{1}=(1-n)\vee(1-n+x_{1}-x_{2})}^{(n-1)\wedge(n-1+x_{1}-x_{2})}K\left(\frac{y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)K\left(\frac{-x_{1}+x_{2}+y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)e^{\mathbbm{i}2y_{1}\theta}\cdot\frac{{n-|y_{1}|}}{n}.

Observe first that for
\theta=\pm\pi
or
\theta=0
, we have
e^{\mathbbm{i}2y_{1}\theta}=1,\ y_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}

and for the term
\overline{A}_{2}
with the same argument as
for the term
\widetilde{A}_{2}
, we obtain

\overline{A}_{2}\sim 4\pi|\check{f}(\theta)|^{2}\|K\|_{2}^{2},\ \ \mbox{ where }\ \ \check{f}(\theta)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{x=-\infty}^{\infty}\check{C}(x)e^{-\mathbbm{i}x\theta},\ \ \theta\in\{0,\pm\pi\}.

Suppose now
\theta\not=0
and
\theta\not=\pm\pi
, so that the term
e^{\mathbbm{i}2y_{1}\theta}
is present.
By adding and subtracting a term, we have that
\overline{A}_{2}=D_{1}+D_{2},
where
D_{2}
is defined in (
S.4.14
) below and

\displaystyle\begin{split}D_{1}&:=\frac{1}{\Delta_{n}}\sum_{x_{1},x_{2}=-n+1}^{n-1}\check{C}(x_{1})\overline{\check{C}(x_{2})}e^{-\mathbbm{i}x_{1}\theta}e^{\mathbbm{i}x_{2}\theta}\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt\cdot\sum_{y_{1}=(1-n)\vee(1-n+x_{1}-x_{2})}^{(n-1)\wedge(n-1+x_{1}-x_{2})}K^{2}\left(\frac{y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)e^{\mathbbm{i}2y_{1}\theta}\frac{{n-|y_{1}|}}{n}\\
&=\mathscr{O}\left(\frac{1}{\Delta_{n}}\right).\end{split}

(S.4.13)

Indeed, write

w_{n}(y)=K^{2}\left(\frac{y}{\Delta_{n}}\right)\frac{n-|y|}{n}

and consider, for any
c_{1},c_{2}\in[1,\Delta_{n}]
,

\displaystyle\sum_{y=c_{1}}^{c_{2}}w_{n}(y)e^{\mathbbm{i}2y\theta}(e^{\mathbbm{i}2\theta}-1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y=c_{1}+1}^{c_{2}+1}w_{n}(y-1)e^{\mathbbm{i}2y\theta}-\sum_{y=c_{1}}^{c_{2}}w_{n}(y)e^{\mathbbm{i}2y\theta}

\displaystyle=w_{n}(c_{2})e^{\mathbbm{i}2(c_{2}+1)\theta}-w_{n}(c_{1})e^{\mathbbm{i}2c_{1}\theta}+\sum_{y=c_{1}+1}^{c_{2}}(w_{n}(y-1)-w_{n}(y))e^{\mathbbm{i}2y\theta}.

Focusing on the second term,

\displaystyle\sum_{y=c_{1}+1}^{c_{2}}(w_{n}(y-1)-w_{n}(y))e^{\mathbbm{i}2y\theta}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y=c_{1}+1}^{c_{2}}\left(K^{2}\left(\frac{y-1}{\Delta_{n}}\right)\frac{n-1-(y-1)}{n}-K^{2}\left(\frac{y}{\Delta_{n}}\right)\frac{n-1-y}{n}\right)e^{\mathbbm{i}2y\theta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{y=c_{1}+1}^{c_{2}}K^{2}\left(\frac{y-1}{\Delta_{n}}\right)e^{\mathbbm{i}2y\theta}+\sum_{y=c_{1}+1}^{c_{2}}\left(K^{2}\left(\frac{y-1}{\Delta_{n}}\right)-K^{2}\left(\frac{y}{\Delta_{n}}\right)\right)\frac{n-1-y}{n}e^{\mathbbm{i}2y\theta}

\displaystyle=:E_{1}+E_{2}.

Clearly,
E_{1}={\cal O}(\Delta_{n}/n)=o(1)
uniformly in
c_{1},c_{2}
. Also, it follows that

\displaystyle|E_{2}|\leq\sum_{y=c_{1}+1}^{c_{2}}\left|K^{2}\left(\frac{y-1}{\Delta_{n}}\right)-K^{2}\left(\frac{y}{\Delta_{n}}\right)\right|<\infty\mbox{ uniformly in $c_{1},c_{2}$}

since
K^{2}
is of bounded variation (recall that
K^{\prime}
is bounded and
K
is compactly supported).
Thus,

\displaystyle\sum_{y=c_{1}}^{c_{2}}w_{n}(y)e^{\mathbbm{i}2y\theta}

\displaystyle=(e^{\mathbbm{i}2\theta}-1)^{-1}\left(w_{n}(c_{2})e^{\mathbbm{i}(c_{2}+1)\theta}-w_{n}(c_{1})e^{\mathbbm{i}c_{1}\theta}+\sum_{y=c_{1}+1}^{c_{2}}(w_{n}(y-1)-w_{n}(y))e^{\mathbbm{i}y\theta}\right),

which is uniformly bounded. (Note that here we used the fact that
e^{\mathbbm{i}2\theta}-1\not=0
, since
\pm\pi\not=\theta\not=0
.)
Applying this argument,
we see that the inner sum in (
S.4.13
) is uniformly bounded, and hence by Condition (
S.4.11
),
we obtain that
D_{1}=o(1)
.

On the other hand, for the term
D_{2}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}D_{2}&:=\frac{1}{\Delta_{n}}\sum_{x_{1},x_{2}=-n+1}^{n-1}\check{C}(x_{1})\overline{\check{C}(x_{2})}e^{-\mathbbm{i}x_{1}\theta}e^{\mathbbm{i}x_{2}\theta}\\
&\qquad\times\sum_{y_{1}=(1-n)\vee(1-n+x_{1}-x_{2})}^{(n-1)\wedge(n-1+x_{1}-x_{2})}K\left(\frac{y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)\left[K\left(\frac{-x_{1}+x_{2}+y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)-K\left(\frac{y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)\right]\\
&\hskip 99.58464pt\times e^{\mathbbm{i}2y_{1}\theta}\cdot\frac{{n-|y_{1}|}}{n}\\
&=o(1),\end{split}

(S.4.14)

using the same arguments as for
B_{2}
and
C_{2}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\overline{A}_{2}\to 0\quad\mbox{for}\quad\theta\not=0\ \ \mbox{ and }\ \ \theta\not=\pm\pi.

This completes the derivation for
\mathbb{E}(\mathscr{T}_{n}^{2})
.

The case
k\geq 3

Fix some integer
k\geq 3
. Let
\mathscr{P}
be the set of all possible pairings of
\{i_{\ell},j_{\ell}:\ \ell=1,\ldots,k\}
. Then a pairing
\mathbbm{p}\in\mathscr{P}
iff

\mathbbm{p}=\left\{\{I,J\},\{I,\tilde{I}\},\{J,\tilde{J}\}:I\neq\tilde{I}\in\{i_{\ell},\ell=1,\ldots,k\},J\neq\tilde{J}\in\{j_{\ell},\ell=1,\ldots,k\}\right\},

where all symbols
i_{\ell},j_{\ell},\ell=1,\ldots,k
can be used only once.

By Lemma
S.5.4
,

\displaystyle\mu_{k}

\displaystyle:=(2\pi)^{k}\mathbb{E}(\mathscr{T}_{n}^{k})=\frac{1}{(n\Delta_{n})^{k/2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{\ell},j_{\ell}=1\\
\ell=1,\ldots,k\end{subarray}}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\left[X_{i_{\ell}}\overline{X_{j_{\ell}}}-C(i_{\ell}-j_{\ell})\right]\right]\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}K_{\theta}(i_{\ell}-j_{\ell})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(n\Delta_{n})^{k/2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{\ell},j_{\ell}=1\\
\ell=1,\ldots,k\end{subarray}}^{n}\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}K_{\theta}(i_{\ell}-j_{\ell})

\displaystyle\qquad\times\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbbm{p}\in\mathscr{P}:\\
\cup_{p=1}^{k}\{\{i_{p},j_{p}\}\}\cap\mathbbm{p}=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in\mathbbm{p}}\Big[C(I-J)\mathbbm{1}_{\{i,j\}=\{I,J\}}+\check{C}(I-\tilde{I})\mathbbm{1}_{\{i,j\}=\{I,\tilde{I}\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\qquad\qquad+\overline{\check{C}(J-\tilde{J})}\mathbbm{1}_{\{i,j\}=\{J,\tilde{J}\}}\Big],

Let now
r\leq k
and fix a subset of
2r
indices
\{i_{1}^{\prime},j_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{r}^{\prime},j_{r}^{\prime}\}\subset\{i_{1},j_{1},\cdots,i_{k},j_{k}\}
, where

(i_{1}^{\prime},j_{1}^{\prime})=(i_{\sigma_{1}},j_{\sigma_{1}}),\cdots,(i_{r}^{\prime},j_{r}^{\prime})=(i_{\sigma_{r}},j_{\sigma_{r}})
, for some
1\leq\sigma_{1}<\cdots<\sigma_{r}\leq k
.
A partition of

\{i_{1}^{\prime},j_{1}^{\prime},\ldots i_{r}^{\prime},j_{r}^{\prime}\}
into pairs
will be called a sub-pairing of order
r
. Namely, it is a partition into pairs that involves
r
couples of
i^{\prime}
and/or
j^{\prime}
symbols
taken only from the set
\{i_{1}^{\prime},j_{1}^{\prime},\ldots i_{r}^{\prime},j_{r}^{\prime}\}
.

A (sub)pairing will be called
irreducible
, if does not have further sub-pairings, i.e., it cannot be broken up into a disjoint union of two or more sub-pairings
of lower order. Let
C_{\mathscr{P},r,k}
denote the set of all
irreducible
sub-pairings of order
r
.

Looking at a single summand of the second sum in
\mu_{k},
one can see that every pairing

\mathbbm{p}\in\mathscr{P}
is the union of multiple irreducible pairings of the form
C_{\mathscr{P},r,k}
with
r\geq 2
. We will argue below
that among all pairings in
{\cal P}
only the ones involving irreducible components of order
r=2
contribute asymptotically,
and the remaining pairings are of lower order, as
n\to\infty
.

Let
\mathbbm{p}\in\mathscr{P}
denote a pairing that shows up in the second sum of
(2\pi)^{d}\mathbb{E}(\mathscr{T}_{n}^{k})
, and suppose that

\mathbbm{p}=\mathbbm{p}_{r_{1}}\cup\cdots\cup\mathbbm{p}_{r_{m}},

where the
\mathbbm{p}_{r_{i}}\in C_{\mathscr{P},r_{i},k},\ r_{i}\geq 2,\ i=1,\ldots,m,
are the irreducible sub-pairings of
\mathbbm{p}
.
Then,

\displaystyle\mu_{k}=(2\pi)^{k}\mathbb{E}(\mathscr{T}_{n}^{k})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(n\Delta_{n})^{k/2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{\ell},j_{\ell}=1\\
\ell=1,\ldots,k\end{subarray}}^{n}\Big\{\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}K_{\theta}(i_{\ell}-j_{\ell})

(S.4.15)

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbbm{p}\in{\cal P}:\\
\cup_{p=1}^{k}\left\{\{i_{p},j_{p}\}\right\}\cap\mathbbm{p}=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in\mathbbm{p}}\Big[C(I-J)\mathbbm{1}_{\{i,j\}=\{I,J\}}+\check{C}(I-\tilde{I})\mathbbm{1}_{\{i,j\}=\{I,\tilde{I}\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\overline{\check{C}(J-\tilde{J})}\mathbbm{1}_{\{i,j\}=\{J,\tilde{J}\}}\Big]\Big\}

\displaystyle=:\left(\frac{n}{\Delta_{n}}\right)^{k/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbbm{p}\in{\cal P}\\
\mathbbm{p}=\mathbbm{p}_{r_{1}}\cup\cdots\cup\mathbbm{p}_{r_{m}},\ m\geq 1\end{subarray}}\prod_{t=1}^{m}A_{\mathscr{P},\mathbbm{p}_{r_{t}},k},

where
A_{\mathscr{P},\mathbbm{p}_{r_{t}},k}
involves a product of the terms restricted to the irreducible sub-pairing
\mathbbm{p}_{r_{t}}
, and where

r_{1}+\cdots+r_{m}=k
, with
r_{i}\geq 2,\ i=1,\ldots,m
. Namely,
assuming that the subset of indices
\{i_{1}^{\prime},j_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,i_{r}^{\prime},j_{r}^{\prime}\}=\{i_{\sigma_{2}},j_{\sigma_{1}},\cdots,i_{\sigma_{r}},j_{\sigma_{r}}\},\ r\leq k
, is involved in the irreducible pairing
A_{\mathscr{P},\mathbbm{p}_{r},k}

we have

\displaystyle A_{\mathscr{P},\mathbbm{p}_{r},k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{r}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{\ell}^{\prime},j_{\ell}^{\prime}=1\\
\ell=1,\ldots,r\end{subarray}}^{n}\prod_{\ell=1}^{r}K_{\theta}(i_{\ell}^{\prime}-j_{\ell}^{\prime})\times

\displaystyle\prod_{\{i,j\}\in\mathbbm{p}_{r}}\Big[C(I-J)\mathbbm{1}_{\{i,j\}=\{I,J\}}+\check{C}(I-\tilde{I})\mathbbm{1}_{\{i,j\}=\{I,\tilde{I}\}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\overline{\check{C}(J-\tilde{J})}\mathbbm{1}_{\{i,j\}=\{J,\tilde{J}\}}\Big].

Let
r\geq 3,r\leq k
, and apply the change of variables

\displaystyle x_{\ell}=i-j,\ {i,j}\in\mathbbm{p}_{r}

\displaystyle y_{\ell}=i_{\ell}^{\prime}-j_{\ell}^{\prime},\ell=1,\ldots,r-1,

\displaystyle y_{r}=i_{r}^{\prime},

where the order of
i,j
for
x_{\ell}
is determined by the order they appear in the
C,\check{C}
and
\overline{\check{C}}
terms. Note that since the kernel
K
is non-negative and bounded,

\Big|\prod_{\ell=1}^{r}K_{\theta}(i_{\ell}^{\prime}-j_{\ell}^{\prime})\Big|\leq\|K\|_{\infty}\prod_{\ell=1}^{r-1}K\Big(\frac{y_{\ell}}{\Delta_{n}}\Big).

Then, letting
D(x):=|C(x)|\vee|\check{C}(x)|
, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}&|A_{\mathscr{P},\mathbbm{p}_{r},k}|\\
&\leq\frac{\|K\|_{\infty}}{n^{r}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{\ell}=-n+1\\
\ell=1,\ldots,r\end{subarray}}^{n-1}\prod_{\ell=1}^{r}D(x_{\ell})\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{m}=-n+1\\
m=1,\ldots,r-1\end{subarray}}^{n-1}\prod_{m=1}^{r-1}K\Big(\frac{y_{m}}{\Delta_{n}}\Big)\sum_{y_{r}=1}^{n}1\\
&\leq\frac{\|K\|_{\infty}}{n^{r-1}}\left(\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}D(x)\right)^{r}\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{m}=-n+1\\
m=1,\ldots,r-1\end{subarray}}^{n-1}\prod_{m=1}^{r-1}K\left(\frac{y_{m}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)\\
&=\mathscr{O}\left(\left(\frac{\Delta_{n}}{n}\right)^{r-1}\right).\end{split}

(S.4.16)

where we used that by Relation (
S.4.11
),
\sum_{x}D(x)<\infty
and the compactness of the support of
K
.

Using (
S.4.16
), in view of (
S.4.15
), one immediately has that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\mathscr{T}_{n}^{k})=\mathscr{O}\left(\Big(\frac{n}{\Delta_{n}}\Big)^{k/2}\cdot\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}m=1,\cdots,\lfloor k/2\rfloor\\
r_{1}+\cdots+r_{m}=k,\ r_{t}\geq 2\end{subarray}}\Big(\frac{\Delta_{n}}{n}\Big)^{\sum_{t=1}^{m}(r_{t}-1)}\right)\equiv\mathscr{O}\left(\left(\frac{\Delta_{n}}{n}\right)^{k/2-M}\right),

where

M:=\mathop{\max}_{m=1,\cdots,\lfloor k/2\rfloor}\Big\{m\,:\,r_{1}+\cdots+r_{m}=k,\ \mbox{ where }r_{t}\in\{2,\cdots,k\}\Big\}.

Clearly, if
k
is odd, then
M=(k-1)/2
, we have
k/2-M=1/2
and by the above bound, we obtain

\mathbb{E}[\mathscr{T}_{n}^{k}]={\cal O}((\Delta_{n}/n)^{1/2}),

completing the proof of Proposition
S.4.3
in this case. Note that this moment vanishes as
n\to\infty
.

If
k
is even, then
M=k/2
and
k/2-M=0
. By the above argument, the only pairings that do not vanish asymptotically, as
n\to\infty
,
correspond to
r_{1}=\cdots=r_{k/2}=2
. That is, the indices
\{i_{1},j_{1},\cdots,i_{k},j_{k}\}
are paired into
k/2
irreducible sub-pairings of
order
2
and this case algebraically reduces to the case
k=2
.

Consider four indices
\{i_{1},j_{1},i_{2},j_{2}\}
and let

A_{{\cal P},k}^{(\{i_{1},j_{2}\},\{i_{2},j_{1}\})}
and
A_{{\cal P},k}^{(\{i_{1},i_{2}\},\{j_{1},j_{2}\})}
be the terms of (
S.4.15
) corresponding to the subpairings
\{\{i_{1},j_{2}\},\{i_{2},j_{1}\}\}
and
\{\{i_{1},i_{2}\},\{j_{1},j_{2}\}\}
respectively. Let also

A_{{\cal P},k}^{(\{i_{1},j_{1},i_{2},j_{2}\})}:=A_{{\cal P},k}^{(\{i_{1},j_{2}\},\{i_{2},j_{1}\})}+A_{{\cal P},k}^{(\{i_{1},i_{2}\},\{j_{1},j_{2}\})}

By the first part of the proof, the sum of these two order-2 irreducible subpairings that correspond to the same indices
\{i_{1},j_{1},i_{2},j_{2}\}

contributes the following term to the rate of the expectation:

\Big(\frac{n}{\Delta_{n}}\Big)^{1}A_{{\cal P},k}^{(\{i_{1},j_{1},i_{2},j_{2}\})}\to\sigma_{f}^{2}(\theta):=\Big(f(\theta)^{2}+1_{\{0,\pm\pi\}}(\theta)|\check{f}(\theta)|^{2}\Big)\|K\|_{2}^{2},

as
n\to\infty
. Therefore, in view of (
S.4.15
),

\displaystyle\mu_{k}=(2\pi)^{k}\mathbb{E}(\mathscr{T}_{n}^{k})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{n}{\Delta_{n}}\right)^{k/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbbm{p}\in{\cal P}\\
\mathbbm{p}=\mathbbm{p}_{r_{1}}\cup\cdots\cup\mathbbm{p}_{r_{m}},\ m\geq 1\end{subarray}}\prod_{t=1}^{m}A_{\mathscr{P},\mathbbm{p}_{r_{t}},k}

\displaystyle\asymp\left(\frac{n}{\Delta_{n}}\right)^{k/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbbm{p}\in{\cal P}\\
\mathbbm{p}=\mathbbm{p}_{r_{1}}\cup\cdots\cup\mathbbm{p}_{r_{k/2}}\\
r_{1}=\ldots=r_{k/2}=2\end{subarray}}\prod_{t=1}^{m}A_{\mathscr{P},\mathbbm{p}_{r_{t}},k}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{n}{\Delta_{n}}\right)^{k/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbbm{q}\in{\cal Q}_{2}\\
\mathbbm{q}=\{\{i_{1}^{\prime},j_{1}^{\prime},i_{2}^{\prime},j_{2}^{\prime}\},\cdots,\\
\{i_{k-1}^{\prime},j_{k-1}^{\prime},i_{k}^{\prime},j_{k}^{\prime}\}\}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell,m=1,\ldots,k\\
\ell\neq m\end{subarray}}\left(A_{{\cal P},k}^{(\{i_{\ell}^{\prime},j_{m}^{\prime}\},\{i_{m}^{\prime},j_{\ell}^{\prime}\})}+A_{{\cal P},k}^{(\{i_{\ell}^{\prime},i_{m}^{\prime}\},\{j_{\ell}^{\prime},j_{m}^{\prime}\})}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{n}{\Delta_{n}}\right)^{k/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbbm{q}\in{\cal Q}_{2}\\
\mathbbm{q}=\{\{i_{1}^{\prime},j_{1}^{\prime},i_{2}^{\prime},j_{2}^{\prime}\},\cdots,\\
\{i_{k-1}^{\prime},j_{k-1}^{\prime},i_{k}^{\prime},j_{k}^{\prime}\}\}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell,m=1,\ldots,k\\
\ell\neq m\end{subarray}}A_{{\cal P},k}^{(\{i_{\ell}^{\prime},j_{\ell}^{\prime},i_{m}^{\prime},j_{m}^{\prime}\})}

\displaystyle\to N_{2}(k)\times\Big[\sigma_{f}^{2}(\theta)\Big]^{k/2},

where
N_{2}(k)
denotes the number of ways one can partition the set
\{i_{1},j_{1},\cdots,i_{k},j_{k}\}
into
k/2
sets of 4 members including both
i
and
j
of the same index, and
{\cal Q}_{2}
denotes the collection of all those partitions.

To complete the proof of Proposition
S.4.3
, it remains to argue that
N_{2}(k)=|{\cal Q}_{2}|=(k-1)!!.
Note that every
q\in{\cal Q}_{2}
is determined by a partition into sets of
4
indices
\{i_{\ell_{1}},j_{\ell_{1}},i_{\ell_{2}},j_{\ell_{2}}\}
from the
2k
symbols
\{i_{1},j_{1},\cdots,i_{k},j_{k}\}
. Thus, determining
the number
N_{2}(k)
is equivalent to counting the number of partitions of the set
\{i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}\}
into

2-
point subsets
\{i_{\ell_{1}},i_{\ell_{2}}\}
. The number of ways to pick the first pair is
\binom{k}{2}
, the second pair
\binom{k-2}{2}
, and so on. Therefore

N_{2}(k)
equals

\frac{1}{(k/2)!}\binom{k}{2}\cdot\binom{k-2}{2}\cdots\binom{2}{2}=(k-1)!!,

where we divide by
(k/2)!
since the order of the subsets
\{i_{\ell_{1}},i_{\ell_{2}}\}
does not matter.
∎

S.4.3.2
The general case

The purpose of this section is to finish the verification of (ii) of Proposition
S.4.2
for a general
\mathbb{H}
under the assumptions of the Proposition.
Recall that we already verified (ii) for the spatial setting
\mathbb{H}=\mathbb{C}
in the previous subsection.
The extension from the scalar to the general case is actually quite straightforward. We illustrate this for the second moment.

Recall that
X_{g_{\ell}}(i)=\langle X(i),g_{\ell}\rangle
.
Denote
(2\pi)^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}a_{\ell}\left\langle\mathscr{T}_{n}g_{\ell},g_{\ell}\right\rangle\right]^{2}
by
A_{n,2}
.
By Isserlis’ formula in Lemma
S.5.3
,

\displaystyle A_{n,2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}=1}^{m}a_{\ell_{1}}a_{\ell_{2}}\frac{1}{n\Delta_{n}}\sum_{i_{1},j_{1}=1}^{n}\sum_{i_{2},j_{2}=1}^{n}K_{\theta}(i_{1}-j_{1})K_{\theta}(i_{2}-j_{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\times\mathbb{E}\Big\{\left[X_{g_{\ell_{1}}}(i_{1})\overline{X_{g_{\ell_{1}}}(j_{1})}-\mathbb{E}X_{g_{\ell_{1}}}(i_{1})\overline{X_{g_{\ell_{1}}}(j_{1})}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\times\left[X_{g_{\ell_{2}}}(i_{2})\overline{X_{g_{\ell_{2}}}(j_{2})}-\mathbb{E}X_{g_{\ell_{2}}}(i_{2})\overline{X_{g_{\ell_{2}}}(j_{2})}\right]\Big\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}=1}^{m}a_{\ell_{1}}a_{\ell_{2}}\frac{1}{n\Delta_{n}}\sum_{i_{1},j_{1}=1}^{n}\sum_{i_{2},j_{2}=1}^{n}K_{\theta}(i_{1}-j_{1})K_{\theta}(i_{2}-j_{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\times\Big[\mathbb{E}X_{g_{\ell_{1}}}(i_{1})\overline{X_{g_{\ell_{1}}}(j_{1})}X_{g_{\ell_{2}}}(i_{2})\overline{X_{g_{\ell_{2}}}(j_{2})}

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt-\mathbb{E}X_{g_{\ell_{1}}}(i_{1})\overline{X_{g_{\ell_{1}}}(j_{1})}\mathbb{E}X_{g_{\ell_{2}}}(i_{2})\overline{X_{g_{\ell_{2}}}(j_{2})}\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}=1}^{m}a_{\ell_{1}}a_{\ell_{2}}\frac{1}{n\Delta_{n}}\sum_{i_{1},j_{1}=1}^{n}\sum_{i_{2},j_{2}=1}^{n}K_{\theta}(i_{1}-j_{1})K_{\theta}(i_{2}-j_{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\times\mathbb{E}X_{g_{\ell_{1}}}(i_{1})\overline{X_{g_{\ell_{2}}}(j_{2})}\mathbb{E}X_{g_{\ell_{2}}}(i_{2})\overline{X_{g_{\ell_{1}}}(j_{1})}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}=1}^{m}a_{\ell_{1}}a_{\ell_{2}}\frac{1}{n\Delta_{n}}\sum_{i_{1},j_{1}=1}^{n}\sum_{i_{2},j_{2}=1}^{n}K_{\theta}(i_{1}-j_{1})K_{\theta}(i_{2}-j_{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\times\mathbb{E}X_{g_{\ell_{1}}}(i_{1})X_{g_{\ell_{2}}}(i_{2})\mathbb{E}\overline{X_{g_{\ell_{1}}}(j_{1})}\overline{X_{g_{\ell_{2}}}(j_{2})}

\displaystyle=:\widetilde{A}_{n,2}+\overline{A}_{n,2}.

Define

\displaystyle C_{g_{\ell_{1}},g_{\ell_{2}}}(t):=\mathbb{E}X_{g_{\ell_{1}}}(t)\overline{X_{g_{\ell_{2}}}(0)}.

(S.4.17)

Start with
\theta\not\in\{0,\pm\pi\}
. By the same arguments as in Proposition
S.4.3
, one can focus only on
\widetilde{A}_{2}.
By the change of variables

\displaystyle x_{1}=i_{1}-j_{2},

\displaystyle\ \ x_{2}=i_{2}-j_{1},

\displaystyle y_{1}=i_{1}-j_{1},

\displaystyle\ \ y_{2}=i_{1},

we have that

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}_{n,2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}=1}^{m}a_{\ell_{1}}a_{\ell_{2}}\frac{1}{n\Delta_{n}}\sum_{x_{1},x_{2}=-n+1}^{n-1}C_{g_{\ell_{1}},g_{\ell_{2}}}(x_{1})\overline{C_{g_{\ell_{1}},g_{\ell_{2}}}(-x_{2})}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\sum_{y_{1}=(1-n)\vee(1-n+x_{1}+x_{2})}^{(n-1)\wedge(n-1+x_{1}+x_{2})}K_{\theta}(y_{1})K_{\theta}(x_{1}+x_{2}-y_{1})\sum_{y_{2}=1\vee(1+y_{1})}^{n\wedge(n+y_{1})}1

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}=1}^{m}a_{\ell_{1}}a_{\ell_{2}}\sum_{x_{1},x_{2}=-n+1}^{n-1}C_{g_{\ell_{1}},g_{\ell_{2}}}(x_{1})e^{\mathbbm{i}x_{1}\theta}{\overline{C_{g_{\ell_{1}},g_{\ell_{2}}}(-x_{2})e^{-\mathbbm{i}x_{2}\theta}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\frac{1}{\Delta_{n}}\sum_{y_{1}=(1-n)\vee(1-n+x_{1}+x_{2})}^{(n-1)\wedge(n-1+x_{1}+x_{2})}K(y_{1})K(x_{1}+x_{2}-y_{1})\frac{{n-|y_{1}|}}{n}

\displaystyle\sim\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}=1}^{m}a_{\ell_{1}}a_{\ell_{2}}4\pi^{2}\int_{y=-1}^{1}K^{2}(y)dy\Big|\sum_{x=-\infty}^{\infty}C_{g_{\ell_{1}},g_{\ell_{2}}}(x)e^{\mathbbm{i}x\theta}\Big|^{2}

based on the proof of Proposition
S.4.3
. By (
S.4.17
), this is precisely

\displaystyle 4\pi^{2}\int_{y=-1}^{1}K^{2}(y)dy\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}=1}^{m}a_{\ell_{1}}a_{\ell_{2}}|\left\langle f(\theta)g_{\ell_{1}},g_{\ell_{2}}\right\rangle|^{2}.

The derivations for
\overline{A}_{n,2}
are similar.
For instance, consider
\theta=0
:

\displaystyle\overline{A}_{n,2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n\Delta_{n}}\sum_{i_{1},j_{1}=1}^{n}\sum_{i_{2},j_{2}=1}^{n}K_{0}(i_{1}-j_{1})K_{0}(i_{2}-j_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\times\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}=1}^{m}a_{\ell_{1}}a_{\ell_{2}}\check{C}_{g_{\ell_{1}},g_{\ell_{2}}}(i_{1}-i_{2})\overline{\check{C}_{g_{\ell_{1}},g_{\ell_{2}}}(j_{1}-j_{2})},

where

\displaystyle\check{C}_{g_{\ell_{1}},g_{\ell_{2}}}(t):=\mathbb{E}[X_{g_{\ell_{1}}}(t)X_{g_{\ell_{2}}}(0)]=\left\langle\mathbb{E}\left[X(t)\otimes\overline{X(0)}\right]\overline{g_{\ell_{2}}},g_{\ell_{1}}\right\rangle=\langle\check{C}(t)\overline{g_{\ell_{2}}},g_{\ell_{1}}\rangle.

Making the change of variables

\displaystyle x_{1}=i_{1}-i_{2},

\displaystyle\ \ x_{2}=j_{1}-j_{2},

\displaystyle y_{1}=i_{1}-j_{1},

\displaystyle\ \ y_{2}=i_{1},

we have that

\displaystyle\overline{A}_{n,2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}=1}^{m}a_{\ell_{1}}a_{\ell_{2}}\frac{1}{\Delta_{n}}\sum_{x_{1},x_{2}=1-n}^{n-1}\check{C}_{g_{\ell_{1}},g_{\ell_{2}}}(x_{1})\overline{\check{C}_{g_{\ell_{1}},g_{\ell_{2}}}(x_{2})}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\sum_{y_{1}=(1-n)\vee(1-n+x_{1}-x_{2})}^{(n-1)\wedge(n-1)+x_{1}-x_{2}}K\left(\frac{y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)K\left(\frac{-x_{1}+x_{2}+y_{1}}{\Delta_{n}}\right)\frac{n-|y_{1}|}{n}

\displaystyle\sim 4\pi^{2}\int_{y=-1}^{1}K^{2}(y)dy\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}=1}^{m}a_{\ell_{1}}a_{\ell_{2}}\left|\left\langle\check{f}(0)\overline{g_{\ell_{2}}},g_{\ell_{1}}\right\rangle\right|^{2},

using again the arguments of the proof of Proposition
S.4.3
.
Thus, we have verified (ii) of Proposition
S.4.2
for
k=2
.

In a similar manner, the derivation of
\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}a_{\ell}\left\langle\mathscr{T}_{n}g_{\ell},g_{\ell}\right\rangle\right]^{k}
for
k\geq 3
for a general space
\mathbb{H}
can be extended from that for the scalar case, and the details are omitted.

S.5
Cumulants and Isserlis’ formulas

S.5.1
Cumulants for functional data

This section is of independent interest and provides an extension of Isserlis’ theorem to the regime of Hilbert space valued Gaussiaan random variables. We start by providing the definition of the cumulants for scalar random variables taking values in
\mathbb{R}
.

Definition S.5.1
.

Let
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{k}
be random variables taking values in
\mathbb{R}
such that
\mathbb{E}(\prod_{j\in B}Y_{j})
is well defined and finite for all subsets
B
of
\{1,\ldots,k\}
. Then,

{\rm cum}\left(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{k}\right):=\sum_{\nu=(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{q})}(-1)^{q-1}(q-1)!\prod_{l=1}^{q}\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j\in\nu_{l}}Y_{j}\right],

where the sum is over all unordered partitions of
\{1,\ldots,k\}.

The following lemma follows from the discussion on page 34 of
Rosenblatt 1985
.

Lemma S.5.1
.

Let
Y_{i},i=1,\ldots,k
be real random variables such that
\mathbb{E}(\prod_{j\in B}Y_{j})
is well defined and finite for all subsets
B
of
\{1,\ldots,k\}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Y_{1}\cdot\ldots\cdot Y_{k}]=\sum_{\nu=(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{p})}\prod_{l=1}^{p}{\rm cum}(Y_{i};\ i\in\nu_{l}),

where the sum is over all the unordered partitions of
\{1,\ldots,k\}.

Proposition S.5.2
.

Let
\{X(t)\}
be a stochastic process taking values in a Hilbert space
\mathbb{H}
, where
\mathbb{E}(\|X(t)\|^{4})<\infty
for all
t
. Note that we
do not assume here
X
to be real. Fix an arbitrary real CONS
\{e_{i},\ i\in I\}
of
\mathbb{H}
and denote by

X_{i}(t):=\langle X(t),e_{i}\rangle
. Then for any
t,s,w,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have that

\displaystyle{\rm cum}\left(X(t),X(s),X(w),X(v)\right)=\sum_{i}\sum_{j}{\rm cum}(X_{j}(t),\overline{X_{i}(s)},\overline{X_{j}(w)},X_{i}(v)).

Recall the definition of cumulant in (
4.1
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\rm cum}\left(X(t),X(s),X(w),X(v)\right)\\
&=\mathbb{E}\left\langle X(t)\otimes X(s),X(w)\otimes X(v)\right\rangle_{\rm HS}-\langle\mathbb{E}(X(t)\otimes X(s)),\mathbb{E}(X(w)\otimes X(v))\rangle_{\rm HS}\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt-\mathbb{E}\left\langle X(t),X(w)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left\langle X(v),X(s)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt-\left\langle\mathbb{E}(X(t)\otimes\overline{X(v)}),\mathbb{E}(X(w)\otimes\overline{X(s)})\right\rangle_{\rm HS}.\end{split}

For any
x(1),\ldots,x(4)\in\mathbb{H}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle x(1)\otimes x(2),x(3)\otimes x(4)\rangle_{\rm HS}&=\sum_{i}\langle(x(1)\otimes x(2))e_{i},(x(3)\otimes x(4))e_{i}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}\\
&=\left\langle x(1),x(3)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}\overline{\left\langle x(2),x(4)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}}\\
&=\sum_{i}\sum_{j}x_{i}(1)\overline{x_{i}(3)x_{j}(2)}x_{j}(4).\end{split}

It follows that

\displaystyle\left\langle X(t)\otimes X(s),X(w)\otimes X(v)\right\rangle_{\rm HS}=\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\overline{X_{i}(s)}X_{i}(v)X_{j}(t)\overline{X_{j}(w)}.

It suffices to show that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\langle X(t)\otimes X(s),X(w)\otimes X(v)\right\rangle_{\rm HS}=\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\mathbb{E}(\overline{X_{i}(s)}X_{i}(v)X_{j}(t)\overline{X_{j}(w)}),

where the interchange of the order of summation and expectation can be justified by the fourth-moment assumption on the
X(t)
and
Fubini’s Theorem.

Similarly, we have that

\langle\mathbb{E}(X(t)\otimes X(s)),\mathbb{E}(X(w)\otimes X(v))\rangle_{\rm HS}=\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\mathbb{E}(\overline{X_{i}(s)}X_{j}(t))\mathbb{E}(X_{i}(v)\overline{X_{j}(w)})

and

\langle\mathbb{E}(X(t)\otimes\overline{X(v)}),\mathbb{E}(X(w)\otimes\overline{X(s)})\rangle_{\rm HS}=\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\mathbb{E}(X_{j}(t)X_{i}(v))\mathbb{E}(\overline{X_{i}(s)}\overline{X_{j}(w)}),

where we used the fact that the CONS
\{e_{j}\}
is real in order to write

\overline{X(s)}=\sum_{i}\overline{X_{i}(s)}e_{j}.

Finally,

\mathbb{E}\left\langle X(t),X(w)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left\langle X(v),X(s)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}=\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\mathbb{E}X_{j}(t)\overline{X_{j}(w)}\mathbb{E}X_{i}(v)\overline{X_{i}(s)}.

Gathering all four terms one can easily see that the cumulant sum

\sum_{i}\sum_{j}{\rm cum}(X_{j}(t),\overline{X_{i}(s)},\overline{X_{j}(w)},X_{i}(v))

is reconstructed.
∎

We end this subsection with a remark on the connection with a related but different notion of
cumulant employed in
Panaretos and
Tavakoli 2013
.

Remark S.5.1
.

Panaretos and
Tavakoli 2013
defines a notion of cumulant on the bottom of
page 571 of the paper. In this remark, we will attempt to explain the connection between the condition
C(0,4)

in
Panaretos and
Tavakoli 2013
with (c) of Assumption
5
.

For simplicity, we shall work with real Hilbert spaces. Recall that in
Panaretos and
Tavakoli 2013
, the authors
consider
\mathbb{H}=L^{2}[0,1]
and define the so-called
cumulant kernel
:

{\rm cum}_{\rm ker}(X(t_{1}),\cdots,X(t_{k})):=\sum_{\nu=(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{p})}(-1)^{p-1}(p-1)!\prod_{\ell=1}^{p}\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod_{j\in\nu_{\ell}}X(\tau_{j};t_{j})\Big],

where
X(t):=(X(\tau;t),\ \tau\in[0,1])\in L^{2}([0,1])
. For a kernel of order
2k
, one can define the so-called cumulant
operator
{\cal R}:L^{2}([0,1]^{k})\to L^{2}([0,1]^{k})
, as

{\cal R}(h):=\int_{[0,1]^{2}}{\rm cum}_{\rm ker}(X(t_{1}),\cdots,X(t_{2k}))(\tau_{1},\cdots,\tau_{2k})h(\tau_{k+1},\cdots,\tau_{2k})d\tau_{k+1}\cdots d\tau_{2k},

where the latter is understood as a function of
(\tau_{1},\cdots,\tau_{k})
that can be shown to belong to
L^{2}([0,1]^{k})
.

Fixing a CONS
\{e_{j}\}
of
L^{2}([0,1])
, for
k=2
, we obtain that

{\rm cum}_{\rm ker}(X(t_{1}),\cdots,X(t_{4}))=\sum_{i,j,k,\ell}{\rm cum}(X_{i}(t_{1}),X_{j}(t_{2}),X_{k}(t_{3}),X_{\ell}(t_{4}))e_{i}\otimes e_{j}\otimes e_{k}\otimes e_{\ell},

where
{\rm cum}
stands for the usual cumulant of random variables, and where
X_{i}(t)=\langle X(t),e_{i}\rangle
are the coordinates
of
X(t)
in the basis
\{e_{j}\}
.
Thus, in the basis
\{e_{i}\otimes e_{j}\}
of
L^{2}([0,1]^{2}]\equiv L^{2}([0,1])\otimes L^{2}([0,1])
,
one can view the cumulant operator
{\cal R}:L^{2}([0,1])\otimes L^{2}([0,1])\to L^{2}([0,1])\otimes L^{2}([0,1])
as

{\cal R}=\sum_{i,j,k,\ell}r_{(i,j),(k,\ell)}(e_{i}\otimes e_{j})\otimes(e_{k}\otimes e_{\ell}),

where
r_{(i,j),(k,\ell)}:={\rm cum}(X_{i}(t_{1}),X_{j}(t_{2}),X_{k}(t_{3}),X_{\ell}(t_{4}))
.

From this perspective, by (
4.9
), we obtain that our notion of a cumulant coincides with the trace of the Hilbert-Schmidt
cumulant operator
{\cal R}
:

{\rm trace}({\cal R})={\rm cum}(X(t_{1}),X(t_{2}),X(t_{3}),X(t_{4}))=\sum_{i,j}r_{(i,j),(i,j)}.

On the other hand, the norm of the cumulant kernel employed in the C(0,4) condition of
Panaretos and
Tavakoli 2013
becomes:

\|{\rm cum}_{\rm ker}(X(t_{1}),\cdots,X(t_{4}))\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\sum_{i,j,k,\ell}{\rm cum}(X_{i}(t_{1}),X_{j}(t_{2}),X_{k}(t_{3}),X_{\ell}(t_{4}))^{2}.

Whereas, recall that

{\rm cum}(X(t_{1}),\cdots,X(t_{4}))=\sum_{i,j,k,\ell}{\rm cum}(X_{i}(t_{1}),X_{j}(t_{2}),X_{k}(t_{3}),X_{\ell}(t_{4})).

Thus, the condition
C(0,4)
of
Panaretos and
Tavakoli 2013
that

\sum_{t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}}\|{\rm cum}_{\rm ker}(X(t_{1}),X(t_{2}),X(t_{3}),X(0))\|_{L^{2}}<\infty

is neither strictly weaker nor stronger than our condition (c) in Assumption
5
.

S.5.2
Isserlis’ formulas

The following is an extension of the classical Isserlis’ formula to univariate complex Gaussian variables.

Lemma S.5.3
.

Let
Z_{j}=X_{j}+\mathbbm{i}Y_{j},\ j=1,2,\cdots
be zero-mean, complex jointly Gaussian random variables. That is,
X_{j},Y_{j},\ j=1,2,\cdots
are zero-mean jointly Gaussian
\mathbb{R}
-valued
random variables. Then, for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
\mathbb{E}[\prod_{i=1}^{2m-1}Z_{j}]=0
, and

\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{2m}Z_{j}\right)=\sum_{\pi}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}(Z_{a_{\pi,i}}Z_{b_{\pi,i}}),

where a pairing
\pi
refers to a decomposition of
\{1,\ldots,2m\}
into
m
pairs, which are denoted as
(a_{\pi,i},b_{\pi,i}),i=1,\ldots,m
.

Recall that
Z_{j}=X_{j}+\mathbbm{i}Y_{j}
, where
X_{i},Y_{i}
are real.
Let
\sigma_{a,b}^{(0,0)}=\mathbb{E}(X_{a}X_{b}),\sigma_{a,b}^{(0,1)}=\mathbb{E}(X_{a}Y_{b}),\sigma_{a,b}^{(1,0)}=\mathbb{E}(Y_{a}X_{b}),\sigma_{a,b}^{(1,1)}=\mathbb{E}(Y_{a}Y_{b})
.
Write

\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{2m}(X_{j}+\mathbbm{i}Y_{j})\right)=\sum_{S\subset\{1,\ldots,2m\}}\mathbbm{i}^{|S|}\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{j\not\in S}X_{j}\prod_{k\in S}Y_{k}\right).

By the Isserlis formula for real Gaussian random variables introduced by
Isserlis 1918
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{j\not\in S}X_{j}\prod_{k\in S}Y_{k}\right)=\sum_{\pi}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\sigma_{a_{\pi,i},b_{\pi,i}}^{(\mathbbm{1}(a_{\pi,i}\in S),\mathbbm{1}(b_{\pi,i}\in S))},

and hence

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{2m}(X_{j}+\mathbbm{i}Y_{j})\right)=\sum_{\pi}\sum_{S\subset\{1,\ldots,2m\}}\mathbbm{i}^{|S|}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\sigma_{a_{\pi,i},b_{\pi,i}}^{(\mathbbm{1}(a_{\pi,i}\in S),\mathbbm{1}(b_{\pi,i}\in S))}.

For any given
\pi
and
S
, we let
\alpha_{i}=\mathbbm{1}(a_{\pi,i}\in S),\beta_{i}=\mathbbm{1}{(b_{\pi,i}\in S)}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{2m}(X_{j}+\mathbbm{i}Y_{j})\right)&=\sum_{\pi}\sum_{{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}=0,1\\
i=1,\ldots,m\end{subarray}}}\mathbbm{i}^{\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}+\sum_{i}\beta_{i}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\sigma_{a_{\pi,i},b_{\pi,i}}^{(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})}\\
&=\sum_{\pi}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}=0,1}\mathbbm{i}^{\alpha_{i}+\beta_{i}}\sigma_{a_{\pi,i},b_{\pi,i}}^{(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})}\\
&=\sum_{\pi}\prod_{i=1}^{m}(1,\mathbbm{i})C(a_{\pi,i},b_{\pi,i})(1,\mathbbm{i})^{\top},\end{split}

(S.5.1)

where

\displaystyle C(a,b)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbb{E}(X_{a}X_{b})&\mathbb{E}(X_{a}Y_{b})\\
\mathbb{E}(X_{b}Y_{a})&\mathbb{E}(Y_{a}Y_{b})\end{array}\right).

Notice that
\mathbb{E}[Z_{a}Z_{b}]=(1,\mathbbm{i})C(a,b)(1,\mathbbm{i})^{\top}
and thus the right-hand side of (
S.5.1
) equals

\sum_{\pi}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}(Z_{a_{\pi,i}}Z_{b_{\pi,i}}),

which shows that the Isserlis formula for complex-valued r.v.’s is exactly the same as that for real-valued random variables.
∎

Lemma S.5.4
.

Let
\{X(t),t\in\mathbb{R}\}
be a stationary Gaussian process in
\mathbb{C}
with
C(t-s)=\mathbb{E}X(t)\overline{X(s)}
and
\check{C}(t-s)=\mathbb{E}X(t)X(s)
.
Consider
X(t_{i}),X(s_{i})
,
i=1,\ldots,N+M
for some
N,M\in\mathbb{Z}
, with

N\geq 0
and
M\geq 0
. Denote by
\mathscr{P}_{N,M}
the class of all pairings of the set

\{t_{i},s_{i}|i=1,\ldots,N+M\}

and by
\mathscr{P}_{N,M,-k}
the class of all pairings of

\{t_{i},s_{i}|i=1,\ldots,N+M\}\setminus\{t_{k},s_{k}\}.

This means that
\tilde{u}\in\mathscr{P}_{N,M}
iff

\tilde{u}=\left\{\left\{\tau,\sigma\right\},\left\{\tau,\tilde{\tau}\right\},\left\{\sigma,\tilde{\sigma}\right\}|\tau\neq\tilde{\tau}\in\{t_{i}:i=1,\ldots,N+M\},\ \sigma\neq\tilde{\sigma}\in\{s_{i}:i=1,\ldots,N+M\}\right\}

and each symbol
t_{i},s_{i},\ i=1,\ldots,N+M
can be used only once.
Then,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{n=1}^{N}X(t_{n})\overline{X(s_{n})}\cdot\prod_{m=1}^{M}\Big(X(t_{N+m})\overline{X(s_{N+m})}-C(t_{N+m}-s_{N+m})\Big)\right]\\
&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{u}\in\mathscr{P}_{N,M}:\\
\cup_{m=1}^{M}\left\{\{t_{N+m},s_{N+m}\}\right\}\cap\tilde{u}=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in\tilde{u}}\Big[C(\tau-\sigma)\mathbbm{1}_{\{i,j\}=\{\tau,\sigma\}}+\check{C}(\tau-\tilde{\tau})\mathbbm{1}_{\{i,j\}=\{\tau,\tilde{\tau}\}}\\
&\hskip 142.26378pt+\overline{\check{C}(\sigma-\tilde{\sigma})}\mathbbm{1}_{\{i,j\}=\{\sigma,\tilde{\sigma}\}}\Big].\end{split}

(S.5.2)

This Isserlis-type result is used in the proof of Proposition
S.4.3
(see e.g. (
S.4.15
)),
where the
k
th order moments of the spectral density estimators involve terms as in (
S.5.2
) where
N=0
. The reason we formulate (
S.5.2
)
for general
N\geq 0
is to facilitate the proof of this relation by the method of induction.

We will prove the desired equality by using induction on
N+M
.
When
N+M=1,
the equality holds trivially.
For the basis of our induction, we use
N+M=2
. We look at the three different cases.

(a)

N=2
. The equality trivially holds by the Isserlis’ formula.

(b)

N=M=1
. We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle\left[X(t_{1})\overline{X(s_{1})}\cdot(X(t_{2})\overline{X(s_{2})}-C(t_{2}-s_{2}))\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[X(t_{1})\overline{X(s_{1})}X(t_{2})\overline{X(s_{2})}\right]-C(t_{1}-s_{1})C(t_{2}-s_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[X(t_{1})\overline{X(s_{1})}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[X(t_{2})\overline{X(s_{2})}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[X(t_{1})X(t_{2})\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{X(s_{1})}\overline{X(s_{2})}\right]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\left[X(t_{1})\overline{X(s_{2})}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{X(s_{1})}X(t_{2})\right]-C(t_{1}-s_{1})C(t_{2}-s_{2})

\displaystyle=\check{C}(t_{1}-t_{2})\overline{\check{C}(s_{1}-s_{2})}+C(t_{1}-s_{2})C(t_{2}-s_{1})

where Isserlis’ formula was used in the second equality.

(c)

M=2
. We have that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}\left[(X(t_{1})\overline{X(s_{1})}-C(t_{1}-s_{1}))\cdot(X(t_{2})\overline{X(s_{2})}-C(t_{2}-s_{2}))\right]\\
&=\mathbb{E}\left[X(t_{1})\overline{X(s_{1})}X(t_{2})\overline{X(s_{2})}\right]-C(t_{1}-s_{1})C(t_{2}-s_{2})\\
&=\check{C}(t_{1}-t_{2})\overline{\check{C}(s_{1}-s_{2})}+C(t_{1}-s_{2})C(t_{2}-s_{1})\end{split}

(S.5.3)

similarly to case
(b)
.

For the induction hypothesis, we assume that the desired equality holds when
N+M=r.

Let now
N+M=r+1.
We discern two cases.

(a)

N=r+1
. The result follows directly by Isserlis’ formula.

(b)

N<r+1
. The following holds

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{n=1}^{N}X(t_{n})\overline{X(s_{n})}\cdot\prod_{m=1}^{M}\Big(X(t_{N+m})\overline{X(s_{N+m})}-C(t_{N+m}-s_{N+m})\Big)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{n=1}^{N+1}X(t_{n})\overline{X(s_{n})}\prod_{m=2}^{M}\Big(X(t_{N+m})\overline{X(s_{N+m})}-C(t_{N+m}-s_{N+m})\Big)\right]

\displaystyle\quad-C(t_{N+1}-s_{N+1})\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{n=1}^{N}X(t_{n})\overline{X(s_{n})}\cdot\prod_{m=2}^{M}\Big(X(t_{N+m})\overline{X(s_{N+m})}-C(t_{N+m}-s_{N+m})\Big)\right]

\displaystyle=\ldots

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{n=1}^{N+M}X(t_{n})\overline{X(s_{n})}\right]

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{m=1}^{M}C(t_{N+m}-s_{N+m})\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod_{n=1}^{N+m-1}X(t_{n})\overline{X(s_{n})}

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\cdot\prod_{k=m+1}^{M}\Big(X(t_{N+k})\overline{X(s_{N+k})}-C(t_{N+k}-s_{N+m})\Big)\Big]

Applying Isserlis’ formula the first summand is equal to

\displaystyle\sum_{\tilde{u}\in\mathscr{P}_{N,M}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in\tilde{u}}\Big[C(\tau-\sigma)\mathbbm{1}_{\{i,j\}=\{\tau,\sigma\}}+\check{C}(\tau-\tilde{\tau})\mathbbm{1}_{\{i,j\}=\{\tau,\tilde{\tau}\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\overline{\check{C}(\sigma-\tilde{\sigma})}\mathbbm{1}_{\{i,j\}=\{\sigma,\tilde{\sigma}\}}\Big].

By applying the induction hypothesis in the second summand, since all terms involve
r
factors in total, we have that the second term is equal to

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{M}C(t_{N+m}-s_{N+m})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{u}\in\mathscr{P}_{N,M,-m}:\\
\cup_{i=N+m+1}^{N+M}\left\{\{t_{i},s_{i}\}\right\}\cap\tilde{u}=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in\tilde{u}}\Big[C(\tau-\sigma)\mathbbm{1}_{\{i,j\}=\{\tau,\sigma\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt+\check{C}(\tau-\tilde{\tau})\mathbbm{1}_{\{i,j\}=\{\tau,\tilde{\tau}\}}+\overline{\check{C}(\sigma-\tilde{\sigma})}\mathbbm{1}_{\{i,j\}=\{\sigma,\tilde{\sigma}\}}\Big].

Denote the first term of the previous sum as
A
, the second term as
B
, write
B:=\sum_{m=1}^{M}B_{m}
. Also, let

C_{i,j}^{\tilde{u}}:=C(\tau-\sigma)\mathbbm{1}_{\{i,j\}=\{\tau,\sigma\}}+\check{C}(\tau-\tilde{\tau})\mathbbm{1}_{\{i,j\}=\{\tau,\tilde{\tau}\}}+\overline{\check{C}(\sigma-\tilde{\sigma})}\mathbbm{1}_{\{i,j\}=\{\sigma,\tilde{\sigma}\}}.

Then we have that

\displaystyle A-B_{M}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tilde{u}\in\mathscr{P}_{N,M}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in\tilde{u}}C_{i,j}^{\tilde{u}}-C(t_{N+M}-s_{N+M})\sum_{\tilde{u}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}_{N,M,-M}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in\tilde{u}^{\prime}}C_{i,j}^{\tilde{u}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{u}\in\mathscr{P}_{N,M}:\\
\{t_{N+M},s_{N+M}\}\not\in\tilde{u}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in\tilde{u}}C_{i,j}^{\tilde{u}}.

Similarly

A-B_{M}-B_{M-1}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{u}\in\mathscr{P}_{N,M}:\\
\cup_{i=M-1}^{M}\left\{\{t_{N+i},s_{N+i}\}\right\}\cap\tilde{u}=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in\tilde{u}}C_{i,j}^{\tilde{u}}.

Continuing this way for all terms
B_{j},\ j=1,\ldots,M
, we have that the proof is complete.

∎

S.6
Proofs for Section
8

As in Section
8
,
\mathbb{H}_{n}
denotes the space spanned by
\{R(u,\cdot),u\in D_{n}\}
and
\Pi_{n}
is the projection operator onto
\mathbb{H}_{n}
.
Although the following result is standard, we include it here for the sake of completeness.

Proposition S.6.1
.

Assume that the matrix
{\boldsymbol{R}}_{n}=\{R(u_{n,i},u_{n,j})\}_{i,j=1}^{m_{n}}
is invertible. Let
g\in\mathbb{H}
and
{\boldsymbol{g}}=(g(u_{n,1}),\ldots,g(u_{n,m_{n}}))^{\top}
. Then, the following hold.

(i)

The projection
\tilde{g}=\Pi_{n}g=\sum_{i}c_{i}R(u_{n,i},\cdot)
where
\boldsymbol{c}:=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{m_{n}})^{\top}={\boldsymbol{R}}_{n}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{g}}
,
and
\tilde{g}(u_{n,i})=g(u_{n,i})
for all
u_{n,i}\in D_{n}
. Moreover,
\left\|g-\tilde{g}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}=\|g\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}-{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{R}}_{n}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{g}}
.

(ii)

|\tilde{g}(u)-g(u)|\leq\|g\|_{\mathbb{H}}\inf_{u^{\prime}\in D_{n}}\sqrt{R(u,u)-2R(u,u^{\prime})+R(u^{\prime},u^{\prime})}.

(i)

By the property of projection,

\tilde{g}=\argmin_{h\in\mathbb{H}_{n}}\|g-h\|_{\mathbb{H}}.

For
h=\sum_{i}c_{i}R(u_{n,i},\cdot)
, the reproducing property entails

\|g-h\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}=\|g\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}-2\boldsymbol{c}^{\top}\boldsymbol{g}+\boldsymbol{c}^{\top}\boldsymbol{R}_{n}\boldsymbol{c},

from which we conclude the minimizer
\boldsymbol{c}
is
{\boldsymbol{R}}_{n}^{-1}{\boldsymbol{g}}
.
It then follows that

\tilde{\boldsymbol{g}}:=\left(\tilde{g}(u_{n,1}),\ldots,\tilde{g}(u_{n,m_{n}})\right)^{\top}={\boldsymbol{R}}_{n}\boldsymbol{c}={\boldsymbol{g}}.

(ii)

Applying again the fact that
g-\tilde{g}\perp R(u^{\prime},\cdot)
for all
u^{\prime}\in D_{n}
, we have for any arbitrary
u\in E
,

\tilde{g}(u)-g(u)=\langle\tilde{g}-g,R(u,\cdot)\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}=\langle\tilde{g}-g,R(u,\cdot)-R(u^{\prime},\cdot)\rangle_{\mathbb{H}},\ u^{\prime}\in D_{n}.

By (i) and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality

|\tilde{g}(u)-g(u)|\leq\|g\|_{\mathbb{H}}\inf_{u^{\prime}\in D_{n}}\|R(u,\cdot)-R(u^{\prime},\cdot)\|_{\mathbb{H}},

where

\|R(u,\cdot)-R(u^{\prime},\cdot)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}=R(u,u)-2R(u,u^{\prime})+R(u^{\prime},u^{\prime}).

∎

Proof of Theorem
8.2
:
First,

\displaystyle\|\tilde{f}(\theta)-f(\theta)\|_{\rm HS}

\displaystyle=\|\Pi_{n}f(\theta)\Pi_{n}-f(\theta)\|_{\rm HS}

\displaystyle\leq\|\Pi_{n}f(\theta)\Pi_{n}-\Pi_{n}f(\theta)\|_{\rm HS}+\|\Pi_{n}f(\theta)-f(\theta)\|_{\rm HS}

\displaystyle\leq\|f(\theta)(\Pi_{n}-{\rm I})\|_{\rm HS}+\|(\Pi_{n}-{\rm I})f(\theta)\|_{\rm HS}

\displaystyle=2\|(\Pi_{n}-{\rm I})f(\theta)\|_{\rm HS}

\displaystyle=2\left(\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\nu_{j}^{2}\|(\Pi_{n}-{\rm I})\phi_{j}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

Next, we consider
\|(\Pi_{n}-{\rm I})g\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}=\|\tilde{g}-g\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}
for a function
g\in\mathbb{H}
with a Lipschitz continuous derivative.
The derivation of this depends little on the value of
g(0)
. To simplify notation, let us make the simplification that the Sobolev space contains functions
g
with
g(0)=0
. Thus, we take the kernel as
R(s,t)=s\wedge t
, i.e., the covariance kernel of the standard Brownian motion.
Then the matrix
\boldsymbol{R}_{n}
in (
8.2
) is indeed invertible. By Proposition
S.6.1
,

\displaystyle\|\tilde{g}-g\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}=\|g\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}-\boldsymbol{g}^{\top}\boldsymbol{R}_{n}^{-1}\boldsymbol{g}

(S.6.1)

where
\boldsymbol{g}=(g(u_{n,i}))_{i=1}^{m_{n}}
contains the values of
g
at the
u_{n,i}
.
It follows that
\boldsymbol{R}_{n}
has the Cholesky decomposition

\boldsymbol{R}_{n}=m_{n}^{-1}\boldsymbol{L}_{n}\boldsymbol{L}_{n}^{\top},

(S.6.2)

where
\boldsymbol{L}_{n}
is a lower triangular matrix of
1
’s and has inverse

\boldsymbol{L}_{n}^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0&0&\cdots&0&0\\
-1&1&0&\cdots&0&0\\
0&-1&1&\cdots&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&1&0\\
0&0&0&\cdots&-1&1\end{bmatrix}.

Indeed, by the independence and the stationarity of the increments of the standard Brownian motion
B
, we have

\boldsymbol{Z}=\sqrt{m_{n}}(\boldsymbol{L}_{n}^{-1})^{\top}\boldsymbol{B},

where
\boldsymbol{B}=\Big(B(i/m_{n})-B((i-1)/m_{n})\Big)_{i=1}^{m_{n}}
and
\boldsymbol{Z}\sim{\cal N}(0,\boldsymbol{I}_{m_{n}})
is a standard Normal
random vector. Since
\boldsymbol{R}_{n}=\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{B}\boldsymbol{B}^{\top}]
, we obtain

\boldsymbol{I}_{m_{n}}=m_{n}(\boldsymbol{L}_{n}^{-1})^{\top}\boldsymbol{R}_{n}\boldsymbol{L}_{n}^{-1}
, which yields (
S.6.2
).
Thus,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}^{\top}\boldsymbol{R}_{n}^{-1}\boldsymbol{g}=m_{n}\boldsymbol{g}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{L}_{n}^{-1})^{\top}\boldsymbol{L}_{n}^{-1}\boldsymbol{g}=m_{n}\sum_{i=1}^{m_{n}}(g(i/m_{n})-g((i-1)/m_{n}))^{2},

(S.6.3)

which is a Riemann approximation of
\|g\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}=\int_{0}^{1}(g^{\prime}(t))^{2}dt
(recall
g(0)=0
).
Since
|g^{\prime}(s)-g^{\prime}(t)|\leq C|s-t|
, it follows from (
S.6.1
) and (
S.6.3
) that

\|\tilde{g}-g\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\leq Cm_{n}^{-1}.

Indeed, by the mean value theorem, we have
g(i/m_{n})-g((i-1)/m_{n})=g^{\prime}(\xi_{n,i})m_{n}^{-1}
, for some
\xi_{n,i}\in[(i-1)/m_{n},i/m_{n}]
, and hence

\displaystyle\|g-\widetilde{g}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}(g^{\prime}(t))^{2}dt-\frac{1}{m_{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{m_{n}}(g^{\prime}(\xi_{n,i}))^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m_{n}}\int_{(i-1)/m_{n}}^{i/m_{n}}|g^{\prime}(t)-g^{\prime}(\xi_{n,i})|\cdot|g^{\prime}(t)+g^{\prime}(\xi_{n,i})|dt

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{m_{n}}\left(\int_{0}^{1}|g^{\prime}(t)|dt+\frac{1}{m_{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{m_{n}}|g^{\prime}(\xi_{n,i})|\right)={\cal O}\left(m_{n}^{-1}\right),

where in the last relation we used the fact that the Riemann sum converges to the integral
\int_{0}^{1}|g^{\prime}(t)|dt<\infty
, as
m_{n}\to\infty
.
Applying this bound and by the assumption on the
\phi_{j}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\nu_{j}^{2}\|(\Pi_{n}-{\rm I})\phi_{j}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\leq m_{n}^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}C_{j}\nu_{j}^{2}.

This completes the proof.
∎

S.7
Some properties of the trace norm

We collect some elementary facts of the trace norm in the following lemma.

Lemma S.7.1
.

Let
{\cal A}
be a trace class operator on the Hilbert space
\mathbb{H}
. Then

(i)

\|{\cal A}\|_{\rm tr}=\sup_{{\cal W}:{\rm unitary}}\left|\langle{\cal A},{\cal W}\rangle_{\rm HS}\right|
;

(ii)

\sum_{i}|\langle{\cal A}f_{i},g_{i}\rangle|\leq\|{\cal A}\|_{\rm tr}
for any CONSs
\{f_{i}\}
and
\{g_{i}\}
;

(iii)

\sum_{i}\left|\left\langle{\cal A}e_{i},e_{i}\right\rangle\right|\leq\|{\cal A}\|_{\rm tr}
for any CONS
\{e_{i}\}
.

(i)
Suppose
A
has the SVD

\displaystyle{\cal A}=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}v_{j}\otimes w_{j},

(S.7.1)

where
\lambda_{j}\geq 0
and
\{v_{j}\},\{w_{j}\}
are CONS of
\mathbb{H}
. Then, we can write

{\cal A}=\left(\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}v_{j}\otimes v_{j}\right)\left(\sum_{k}v_{k}\otimes w_{k}\right)=:{\cal PU},

which is a polar decomposition of
{\cal A}
.
It follows that

\|{\cal A}\|_{\rm tr}={\rm trace}({\cal P})={\rm trace}({\cal AU}^{*})=\langle{\cal A},{\cal U}\rangle_{\rm HS}.

Suppose
{\cal W}
is unitary and has the SVD
{\cal W}=\sum_{k}a_{k}\otimes b_{k}
. Then

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left|\langle{\cal A},{\cal W}\rangle_{\rm HS}\right|&=\left|\sum_{k}\langle{\cal A}b_{k},{\cal W}b_{k}\rangle\right|\\
&=\left|\sum_{j}\sum_{k}\lambda_{j}\langle(v_{j}\otimes w_{j})b_{k},a_{k}\rangle\right|\\
&=\left|\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\sum_{k}\langle v_{j},a_{k}\rangle\langle b_{k},w_{j}\rangle\right|\leq\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\end{split}

by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.

(ii) By (
S.7.1
),

\displaystyle\sum_{i}|\langle{\cal A}f_{i},g_{i}\rangle|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}|\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\langle v_{j},g_{i}\rangle\langle w_{j},f_{i}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\sum_{i}|\langle v_{j},g_{i}\rangle\langle w_{j},f_{i}\rangle|,

and the result again follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.

(iii) This is a special case of (ii) with
f_{i}=g_{i}
.

∎

S.8
Examples

In this section, we discuss several concrete examples that illustrate the breadth and scope of the conditions imposed in various results in the paper.

S.8.1
An example of the class
{\cal P}_{D}(\beta,L)

We consider in this section with an example of a class of covariance structures, where the rate of consistency nearly matches the optimal rate of
{\cal P}_{D}(\beta,L).
This class consists of regularly varying covariance structures, as follows.

Example S.8.1
.

Consider
d=1
and the scalar-valued case
\mathbb{H}=\mathbb{C}
. Let

C(k)=|k|^{-\beta-1}S(|h|),\beta>0,k\in\mathbb{Z}

where
S
is a slowly varying function at infinity. It is not hard to see that the corresponding spectral densities
f\in{\cal P}_{D}(\beta+\epsilon,L)
for any
\epsilon>0
, depending on the value of
L
. Also, assume that the kernel function is of the form

K(h)=[1-|h|^{\lambda+1}]_{+},h\in\mathbb{R}

for some
\lambda>0.
We work in the discrete time setting, so we are using the estimator
\hat{f}_{n}(\theta)
.

Thus, we have that

\displaystyle(2\pi)

\displaystyle[f(\theta)-\mathbb{E}\hat{f}_{n}(\theta)]=\sum_{|k|\geq\Delta_{n}}e^{\mathbbm{i}k\theta}C(k)+\sum_{|k|<\Delta_{n}}e^{\mathbbm{i}k\theta}C(k)\left[1-K\left(\frac{k}{\Delta_{n}}\right)\right]

Consider
\theta=0.
Then, the previous expression is equal to

\displaystyle 2\sum_{k\geq\Delta_{n}}k^{-\beta-1}S(k)+2\sum_{k<\Delta_{n}}k^{-\beta-1}S(k)\cdot\frac{k^{\lambda+1}}{\Delta_{n}^{\lambda+1}}

Using the fact that for
p>-1,

\int_{\alpha}^{x}t^{p}S(t)dt\sim(p+1)^{-1}x^{p+1}S(x),\ \text{as}\ x\to\infty

and for
p<-1,

\int_{x}^{\infty}t^{p}S(t)dt\sim|p+1|^{-1}x^{p+1}S(x),\ \text{as}\ x\to\infty

we obtain that for
0<\beta<\min\{1,\lambda+1\}:

\displaystyle(2\pi)[f(0)-\mathbb{E}\hat{f}_{n}(0)]

\displaystyle\sim\frac{\Delta_{n}^{-\beta}S(\Delta_{n})}{\beta}+\frac{\Delta_{n}^{-\beta}S(\Delta_{n})}{\lambda+1-\beta}=\mathscr{O}\left(\Delta_{n}^{-\beta}\cdot S(\Delta_{n})\right)

Also, for
\beta>1,
the same expression can be evaluated to be of the same order

\mathscr{O}\left(\Delta_{n}^{-\beta}\cdot S(\Delta_{n})\right).

Compare this to the rate of Proposition
5.2
for
0<\beta\neq 1
.

We shift our interest now to the variance. At first, using
T
and
\Delta
in place of
T_{n}
and
\Delta_{n}
respectively, we have

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{n}(0)-

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\hat{f}_{n}(0)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\cdot\sum_{t=0}^{T}\sum_{s=0}^{T}K\left(\frac{t-s}{\Delta}\right)\frac{X(t)\cdot X(s)-C(t-s)}{|T-(t-s)|}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\cdot\sum_{v=0}^{T}\sum_{h=-\Delta}^{\Delta}K\left(\frac{h}{\Delta}\right)\frac{X(v+h)\cdot X(v)-C(h)}{|T-h|}\cdot\mathbbm{1}(0\leq h+v\leq T),

by the change of variables
h=t-s,v=s
. Thus,

\displaystyle(2\pi)^{2}\mathbb{E}\left|\hat{f}_{n}(0)-\mathbb{E}\hat{f}_{n}(0)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{v=0}^{T}\sum_{w=0}^{T}\sum_{|h|\leq\Delta}\sum_{|\tilde{h}|\leq\Delta}K\left(\frac{h}{\Delta}\right)K\left(\frac{\tilde{h}}{\Delta}\right)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}\left\{\left[X(v+h)\cdot X(v)-C(h)\right]\left[X\left(w+\tilde{h}\right)\cdot X(w)-C\left(\tilde{h}\right)\right]\right\}}{|T-h|\left|T-\tilde{h}\right|}

\displaystyle\cdot\mathbbm{1}\left(0\leq h+v\leq T\right)\cdot\mathbbm{1}\left(0\leq\tilde{h}+w\leq T\right)

After using Isserlis’ lemma for the expectation in the middle we finally obtain that

\displaystyle(2\pi)^{2}\mathbb{E}\left|\hat{f}_{n}(0)-\mathbb{E}\hat{f}_{n}(0)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T^{2}}\cdot\sum_{v=0}^{T}\sum_{w=0}^{T}\sum_{|h|\leq\Delta}\sum_{|\tilde{h}|\leq\Delta}K\left(\frac{h}{\Delta}\right)K\left(\frac{\tilde{h}}{\Delta}\right)

\displaystyle\frac{C\left(v-w+h-\tilde{h}\right)C(v-w)+C(v-w+h)C\left(w-v+\tilde{h}\right)}{|T-h|\left|T-\tilde{h}\right|}

\displaystyle\cdot\mathbbm{1}(0\leq h+v\leq T)\cdot\mathbbm{1}\left(0\leq\tilde{h}+w\leq T\right).

We have already shown that both of these terms are absolutely of the order
\mathscr{O}\left(\frac{\Delta}{T}\right)
. Hence, showing asymptotic equivalence of just one of these integrals with a term of order
\Delta/T
is enough to show that the variance as a whole is of the same order. We focus on the first summand and have with the change of variables
x=v-w,y=w,z=h-\tilde{h},u=\tilde{h},
that

\displaystyle\sum_{v=0}^{T}\sum_{w=0}^{T}\sum_{|h|\leq\Delta}\sum_{|\tilde{h}|\leq\Delta}K\left(\frac{h}{\Delta}\right)K\left(\frac{\tilde{h}}{\Delta}\right)\frac{C\left(v-w+h-\tilde{h}\right)C(v-w)}{|T-h|\left|T-\tilde{h}\right|}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdot\mathbbm{1}\left(0\leq h+v\leq T\right)\cdot\mathbbm{1}\left(0\leq\tilde{h}+w\leq T\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x=-T}^{T}\sum_{z=-2\Delta}^{2\Delta}C(x+z)C(z)\cdot\sum_{u=-\Delta\vee(-\Delta-z)}^{\Delta\wedge(\Delta-z)}K\left(\frac{z+u}{\Delta}\right)K\left(\frac{u}{\Delta}\right)
